<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove the <i>ℓ</i><sup>1</sup>-Bass conjecture for every discrete group. The Hattori–Stallings trace of every idempotent matrix over the complex <i>ℓ</i><sup>1</sup> group algebra is supported on finitely many conjugacy classes of finite-order elements.

- **claim-main-finite-support** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): Let $G$ be a discrete group, let $q\ge1$, and let $e\in M_q(\ell^1(G))$ be idempotent. There is a finite set $F\subset\mathcal C_{\rm fin}(G)$ such that $\tau_C(e)=0$ for every $C\notin F$. Equivalently, $$\operatorname{HS}^1\bigl(K_0(\ell^1(G))\bigr) \subseteq \bigoplus_{C\in\mathcal C_{\rm fin}(G)}\mathbb C[C].$$
  - Explanation: For an idempotent matrix over the complex l^1 group algebra of any discrete group, the Hattori–Stallings trace coefficient vanishes outside a finite set of finite-order conjugacy classes. This gives the l^1-Bass conjecture, and the finite-support statement is part of the conclusion.
- **claim-trace-tracial** (theorem, [source](#stmt-red-trace)): For every conjugacy class $C$ of a discrete group $G$, the functional $$T_C(a)=\sum_{h\in C}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}a(h),\qquad a\in M_q(\ell^1(G)),$$ is continuous and satisfies $T_C(ab)=T_C(ba)$. It is therefore invariant under similarity by invertible matrices over $\ell^1(G)$.
  - Explanation: The class functional is a continuous trace on matrices over the l^1 group algebra. Hence it is invariant under similarity by invertible matrices.
- **claim-exponential-representative** (theorem, [source](#stmt-red-reduction)): Let $e\in M_q(\ell^1(G))$ satisfy $e^2=e$. There are a finitely generated subgroup $\Gamma\le G$, a word length $\|\cdot\|$ on $\Gamma$, a number $c>0$, and an idempotent $p\in M_q(\ell^1(\Gamma))$ such that $$\label{red:exponential} \sum_{h\in\Gamma}\|p(h)\|_1e^{c\|h\|}<\infty,$$ and $p$ is similar to $e$ in $M_q(\ell^1(G))$. If the trace of every such $p$ has finite support on finite-order conjugacy classes of $\Gamma$, the same conclusion holds for every $e$ over every discrete group.
  - Explanation: An l^1 idempotent is similar to an idempotent with an exponential moment over a finitely generated subgroup. Finite support of the trace for such representatives implies the general case, because passing to the larger group only merges classes.
- **claim-weight-identities** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cy-weights-lemma)): The following properties hold.* 1. *The weights have total sum $\tau_{[g]}(p)$, in every dimension.* 2. *Twisted rotation $(h_0,\ldots,h_k)\mapsto(h_1,\ldots,h_k,gh_0)$ preserves the weights.* 3. *For $k\ge1$, summing the weights over all insertions of one entry into a fixed shortened tuple gives its weight in $\mathcal T_{k-1}(g)$.* *All these sums converge absolutely, even after multiplication by any fixed polynomial in the edge lengths. In particular, with $M_p=\sum_{w\in G}\|p(w)\|_1(1+\|w\|)$, $$\label{cy:moment} \sum_{h\in\mathcal T_k(g)}\|v_{k+1}(h)\|\prod_{i=0}^k l_i \le M_p^{k+1}.$$
  - Explanation: The weights of twisted cyclic tuples sum to the trace coefficient of the class, are invariant under twisted rotation, and are compatible with deleting a vertex. The sums converge absolutely even after polynomial length factors.
- **claim-trace-curvature-average** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cy-trace-identity)): Let $n=2m>0$ and let $\alpha$ be a compatible connection family through dimension $n$. Write $A_h$ for the matrix of $d\alpha_h$ on the section $t_0=0$. If $\mathbb E_{\Delta_n}$ denotes uniform probability on $\Delta_n$, then $$\label{cy:trace-formula} \sum_{h\in\mathcal T_n(g)}v_{n+1}(h)\, \mathbb E_{\Delta_n}\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}(A_h) =(-1)^m\tau_{[g]}(p).$$ The series converges absolutely.
  - Explanation: For any compatible connection family in even dimension, the weighted average of the curvature Pfaffian over the simplex equals the trace coefficient up to sign. The proof compares two families by a transgression form and uses the canonical connection.
- **claim-summary-channels** (construction, [source](#stmt-ch-summary)): Fix $r\ge1$ and $0<\epsilon\le10^{-3}$. There are channels $A,L$ and a cross kernel $\kappa$ with either of the following two choices of radius: the early version has $r'=6r$, and the later version has $r'=r$. In both versions $A$ is labelled by $G$, and for every $P\in\mathcal P_f(G)$ and $y\in G$, $$\begin{aligned} \sum_{z\in G}A(P)_z\bigl(E_r(P,z)-a_*\bigr) &\ge\phi_r(P)-10\epsilon, \label{ch:selection-bound}\\ \operatorname{pred}(P,y) &\le E_{r'}(P,y)+10\epsilon, \label{ch:prediction-upper}\\ \operatorname{pred}(P,y) &\ge E_r(P,y)-10\epsilon \qquad\text{if }A(P)_y>0. \label{ch:prediction-lower} \end{aligned}$$ For $Y=A$ or $Y=L$, derivatives in every transfer direction vanish at zero coordinates and satisfy $$\mathcal F_Y(P;h,h')\le C(1+d(h,h')) \begin{cases} \epsilon^{-10},&\text{early version},\\ r^{10}\epsilon^{-10},&\text{later version}. \end{cases} \label{ch:summary-fisher}$$ Here $C$ depends only on the fixed word metric. The numerical error constant $10$ in <a href="#ch:selection-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:selection-bound">[ch:selection-bound]</a>–<a href="#ch:prediction-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:prediction-lower">[ch:prediction-lower]</a> is independent of the group and of the parameters.
  - Explanation: Smooth equivariant summary channels with a cross kernel predict spatial concentration up to a small additive error, in an early version with enlarged radius and a later version with the original radius. Their Fisher seminorms in transfer directions are controlled.
- **claim-center-geometry** (bound, [source](#stmt-ch-center-geometry)): For $P\in\mathcal P_f(G)$, the set $\{y:E_r(P,y)>1/2\}$ has diameter at most $4r$ and cardinality at most $\exp(\gamma r)$, where $\gamma$ depends only on the word metric.
  - Explanation: Centers carrying more than half the score lie in a set of bounded diameter, so their number is exponentially bounded in the radius, independently of the size of the support.
- **claim-selector-estimates** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ch-selector-estimates)): The formulas <a href="#ch:selector-formulas" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:selector-formulas">[ch:selector-formulas]</a> define smooth equivariant channels. They satisfy $$\sum_y A(P)_y(E_r(P,y)-a_*)\ge\phi_r(P)-3\epsilon. \label{ch:selector-concentration}$$ Whenever $q_r(P)>0.70$, $$\sum_zD(P)_z\ge1-\epsilon, \qquad \sum_zD(P)_zE_r(P,z)\ge q_r(P)-2\epsilon. \label{ch:D-concentration}$$ Both channels have zero derivatives at zero coordinates, and $$\mathcal F_A(P;h,h')+\mathcal F_D(P;h,h') \le C\epsilon^{-2}d(h,h'). \label{ch:selector-fisher}$$
  - Explanation: The exponential selector formulas define smooth equivariant channels that concentrate near a maximal-score center and have Fisher seminorm bounded by a power of the inverse accuracy.
- **claim-terminal-channel** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ch-terminal)): For each $r\ge1$ and $0<\epsilon\le10^{-3}$, the channel $D$ in <a href="#ch:selector-formulas" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:selector-formulas">[ch:selector-formulas]</a> is smooth, left equivariant, and has total mass at most one. Its nonempty support has diameter at most $4r$ and cardinality at most $e^{\gamma r}$, with $\gamma$ depending only on the word metric. If $q_r(P)>0.70$, its mass is at least $1-\epsilon\ge1/2$. Its transfer derivatives vanish at zero coordinates and satisfy $$\mathcal F_D(P;h,h')\le C\epsilon^{-2}d(h,h') \le C\epsilon^{-10}(1+d(h,h')).$$ In particular, if $\phi_r(P)>0$, then $$\sum_zD(P)_z^2\ge\frac14e^{-\gamma r}. \label{ch:terminal-square-mass}$$
  - Explanation: The terminal channel is smooth, equivariant and supported on a bounded set of labels. When concentration is present its squared mass has an exponential lower bound in the radius.
- **claim-accurate-string** (construction, [source](#stmt-ch-accurate-string)): There is a constant $C$, depending only on the word metric, and an integer $b$ depending only on $r,\epsilon$, with $1\le b\le Cr\epsilon^{-4}$, such that for every $P$ and $z$ some $w\in\mathcal A_z^b$ satisfies $\Delta(P;z,w)\le\epsilon$.
  - Explanation: A random-sampling argument shows that a finite string of bounded length reproduces all scores in a bounded region up to a small uniform error.
- **claim-string-weights** (bound, [source](#stmt-ch-string-estimates)): For $M_2$ sufficiently large, uniformly in $P,z,y$, $$\begin{aligned} \sum_w\pi_z(P)_w\Delta(P;z,w)&\le5\epsilon, \label{ch:mean-discrepancy}\\ \left\|\sum_w\pi_z(P)_wH_y(w)-E_r(P^z,y)\right\| &\le5\epsilon. \label{ch:mean-score} \end{aligned}$$ The transfer derivatives satisfy $$\left(\sum_w\frac{\|\dot\pi_z(P)_w\|^2}{\pi_z(P)_w}\right)^{1/2} \le C N_1\frac{d(h,h')}{r}. \label{ch:string-fisher}$$
  - Explanation: Smooth exponential weights on all strings give a small mean discrepancy and mean score error, with transfer derivatives controlled by the weight parameter.
- **claim-form-regularity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-co-regularity)): The forms in <a href="#co:forms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:forms">[co:forms]</a> are well defined, independent of the period cut, and smooth up to every simplex face by one-sided extension. They are invariant under simultaneous translation of all times, under the centralizer action, and under twisted rotation. Their tangential restrictions to faces equal the forms for the shortened tuples, using the same parameters and the same fixed integer $n$.
  - Explanation: The stage forms are well defined, independent of the period cut and smooth up to the faces. They are invariant under the required symmetries and restrict to the forms of shortened tuples.
- **claim-contraction-identity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-co-contraction-lemma)): For every stage $0\leq j\leq b_*$, $$C_j:=B_j(V) =\int_{\rm per}\left( K_j(Y_j(u),Y_j(u)) -\int_{\mathbb R}K_j(Y_j(u),Y_j(u+s))\,d\rho_j(s)\right)du. \label{co:contraction}$$ For $j<b_*$, the second integrated pairing is also $$\int_{\rm per} K_j\bigl((\mu_{j+1}*Y_j)(u),(\mu_{j+1}*Y_j)(u)\bigr)\,du. \label{co:averaged-pairing}$$
  - Explanation: Contracting a stage form with the translation vector gives the same-time pairing minus its lagged average, and for non-final stages the lagged term equals the pairing of the averaged fields.
- **claim-positive-normalizer** (bound, [source](#stmt-co-positivity)): Under <a href="#co:twist-assumption" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:twist-assumption">[co:twist-assumption]</a>, the scalar function $$C_{\rm nor}:=B(V)=\sum_{j=0}^{b_*-1}C_j+WC_{b_*} \label{co:normalizer}$$ satisfies $C_{\rm nor}\geq c_0$, where $c_0=1/10$ is independent of all tuples, $n$, $R$, and $g$.
  - Explanation: The total contraction is bounded below by a fixed positive constant, by telescoping concentration losses across scales and absorbing the raw and terminal stages.
- **claim-connection-family** (theorem, [source](#stmt-co-connection)): Let $R$ be sufficiently large, choose the parameters in <a href="#co:ladder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:ladder">[co:ladder]</a> and <a href="#co:last-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:last-scale">[co:last-scale]</a>, and let $n>10\beta_{b_*}$ be even, with time scales <a href="#co:time-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:time-scales">[co:time-scales]</a>. Suppose that $g$ satisfies <a href="#co:twist-assumption" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:twist-assumption">[co:twist-assumption]</a>. The forms $B_j$ and $B$ in <a href="#co:forms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:forms">[co:forms]</a>, constructed in all dimensions $0\leq k\leq n$ with the same parameters, have the following properties. For every smooth stage $1\leq j\leq b_*$ and every coordinate $t_i$, $$\left(\int_{\rm per}\sum_\omega \frac{\|\partial_iY_j(u)_\omega\|^2}{Y_j(u)_\omega}\,du \right)^{1/2} \leq\frac{a_jl_i}{\sqrt{\delta_j}}. \label{co:fisher}$$ Terms with zero denominator are defined to be zero. Moreover, $$\|B(\partial_i)\|+\|\partial_iC_{\rm nor}\|\leq C_Rl_i. \label{co:coefficients}$$ The scalar $C_{\rm nor}$ is at least $c_0=1/10$, and $$\alpha=\frac{B}{C_{\rm nor}} \label{co:normalized}$$ is a compatible connection family in the sense of Definition <a href="#cy:connection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:connection">5</a>. Its coefficients and those of its exterior derivative are bounded by polynomials in the lengths, uniformly over the tuples. In particular, the family may be used in Proposition <a href="#cy:trace-identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:trace-identity">6</a>.
  - Explanation: Dividing the summed form by its positive contraction yields a compatible connection family with Fisher-type derivative bounds on each smooth stage and polynomial coefficient bounds, so it may be used in the trace identity.
- **claim-pfaffian-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-pf-bound)): Fix a sufficiently large $R$, and set $S=b_*+2$. Let $n$ be an even integer with $n>10\beta_{b_*}$. Suppose either that $g$ has infinite order, or that $g$ has finite order and $$\min_{h\in G}d(h,gh)>4r.$$ For every tuple $(h_0,\ldots,h_n)$, the connection $\alpha=B/C_{\rm nor}$ of Proposition <a href="#co:connection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:connection">17</a> satisfies $$\mathbb E_{\Delta_n}\|\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}(d\alpha)\| \le C_R\bigl(CS^{3/2}R^{-1/10}\bigr)^n \prod_{i=1}^n l_i, \qquad l_i=1+d(h_{i-1},h_i). \label{pf:main-estimate}$$ The constants are independent of $n,g$, and the tuple. Moreover, $S=O(1+\log R)$ as $R\to\infty$.
  - Explanation: The expected absolute curvature Pfaffian decays exponentially in the dimension, with a base that is small for large radius, times the product of edge lengths.
- **claim-order-kernel-approximation** (bound, [source](#stmt-pf-order-kernel)): Let $\mu,\nu$ be finite nonatomic positive measures on $\mathbb R$, and set $M=\mu(\mathbb R)+\nu(\mathbb R)$. Let $A:L^2(\nu)\to L^2(\mu)$ have any one of the kernels $$\operatorname{sign}(s-t),\qquad \mathbf1_{s<t},\qquad \mathbf1_{s>t},$$ where $t$ is the output variable and $s$ the input variable. For each integer $N\ge1$, there is an operator $A_N$ of rank at most $N$ such that $$\\|A-A_N\\|\le \frac{M}{2N}.$$ Also $\\|A\\|\le\sqrt{\mu(\mathbb R)\nu(\mathbb R)}\le M/2$.
  - Explanation: Order kernels between nonatomic measures are approximated in operator norm by finite-rank operators, with an error inversely proportional to the rank.
- **claim-terminal-singular-decay** (bound, [source](#stmt-pf-terminal-singular)): For the operator $T_{b_*}$, every finite-dimensional orthogonal compression has singular values satisfying $$s_\nu\le \frac C\nu,\qquad \nu\ge1. \label{pf:singular-decay}$$ The constant is independent of $n,R,g$, and the tuple, in either of the two cases of Theorem <a href="#pf:bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pf:bound">18</a>.
  - Explanation: Compressions of the terminal operator have singular values decaying like the reciprocal of the index, with a constant independent of dimension, radius, twist and tuple.
- **claim-neighbor-count-average** (bound, [source](#stmt-pf-occupancy)): Let $R\ge1$, $n\ge8R$, and $0\le k\le n$. Choose $(t_1,\ldots,t_n)$ with uniform probability on $\Delta_n$, and independently choose $I$ uniformly among the $k$-element subsets of $\{1,\ldots,n\}$. Then $$\mathbb E_{\Delta_n,I}\prod_{i\in I}N_i(I)^{1/4} \le C^k R^{k/4}, \label{pf:count-average}$$ with the empty product interpreted as one.
  - Explanation: Averaged jointly over the simplex point and a random coordinate subset, the product of fourth roots of neighbor counts is exponentially bounded in the subset size, with a power of the radius.

        {
          "anchor": "stmt-ch-selector-estimates",
          "exact_quote": "The formulas <a href=\"#ch:selector-formulas\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ch:selector-formulas\">[ch:selector-formulas]</a> define smooth equivariant channels. They satisfy $$\\sum_y A(P)_y(E_r(P,y)-a_*)\\ge\\phi_r(P)-3\\epsilon.\n \\label{ch:selector-concentration}$$ Whenever $q_r(P)>0.70$, $$\\sum_zD(P)_z\\ge1-\\epsilon,\n \\qquad\n \\sum_zD(P)_zE_r(P,z)\\ge q_r(P)-2\\epsilon.\n \\label{ch:D-concentration}$$ Both channels have zero derivatives at zero coordinates, and $$\\mathcal F_A(P;h,h')+\\mathcal F_D(P;h,h')\n       \\le C\\epsilon^{-2}d(h,h').\n \\label{ch:selector-fisher}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-207.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "The formulas <a href=\"#ch:selector-formulas\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ch:selector-formulas\">[ch:selector-formulas]</a> define smooth equivariant channels. They satisfy $$\\sum_y A(P)_y(E_r(P,y)-a_*)\\ge\\phi_r(P)-3\\epsilon.\n \\label{ch:selector-concentration}$$ Whenever $q_r(P)>0.70$, $$\\sum_zD(P)_z\\ge1-\\epsilon,\n \\qquad\n \\sum_zD(P)_zE_r(P,z)\\ge q_r(P)-2\\epsilon.\n \\label{ch:D-concentration}$$ Both channels have zero derivatives at zero coordinates, and $$\\mathcal F_A(P;h,h')+\\mathcal F_D(P;h,h')\n       \\le C\\epsilon^{-2}d(h,h').\n \\label{ch:selector-fisher}$$",
      "explanation": "The exponential selector formulas define smooth equivariant channels that concentrate near a maximal-score center and have Fisher seminorm bounded by a power of the inverse accuracy.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "hypotheses": [
        {
          "anchor": "ch:selector-formulas",
          "exact_quote": "Take $N=M_0r\\epsilon^{-2}$ and define $$\\begin{split}\n w^A_y&=\\psi_A(E_r(P,y))^2\n              e^{N(E_r(P,y)-0.80)},\\\\\n w^D_y&=\\psi_D(E_r(P,y))^2\n              e^{N(E_r(P,y)-0.66)},\\\\\n A(P)_y&=\\frac{w^A_y}{1+\\sum_z w^A_z},\\qquad\n D(P)_y=\\frac{w^D_y}{1+\\sum_z w^D_z}.\n \\end{split}\n \\label{ch:selector-formulas}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-207.1"
        },
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-ch-selector-estimates",
          "exact_quote": "Whenever $q_r(P)>0.70",
          "source_id": "PM-207.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "claim-selector-estimates",
      "kind": "theorem",
      "quantifiers": [],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-ch-selector-estimates"
    },
    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-ch-terminal",
        "exact_quote": "For each $r\\ge1$ and $0<\\epsilon\\le10^{-3}$, the channel $D$ in <a href=\"#ch:selector-formulas\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ch:selector-formulas\">[ch:selector-formulas]</a> is smooth, left equivariant, and has total mass at most one. Its nonempty support has diameter at most $4r$ and cardinality at most $e^{\\gamma r}$, with $\\gamma$ depending only on the word metric. If $q_r(P)>0.70$, its mass is at least $1-\\epsilon\\ge1/2$. Its transfer derivatives vanish at zero coordinates and satisfy $$\\mathcal F_D(P;h,h')\\le C\\epsilon^{-2}d(h,h')\n       \\le C\\epsilon^{-10}(1+d(h,h')).$$ In particular, if $\\phi_r(P)>0$, then $$\\sum_zD(P)_z^2\\ge\\frac14e^{-\\gamma r}.\n \\label{ch:terminal-square-mass}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-207.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-ch-accurate-string",
        "exact_quote": "There is a constant $C$, depending only on the word metric, and an integer $b$ depending only on $r,\\epsilon$, with $1\\le b\\le Cr\\epsilon^{-4}$, such that for every $P$ and $z$ some $w\\in\\mathcal A_z^b$ satisfies $\\Delta(P;z,w)\\le\\epsilon$.",
        "source_id": "PM-207.1"
      },

          "source_id": "PM-207.1"
        }

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-ch-string-estimates",
        "exact_quote": "For $M_2$ sufficiently large, uniformly in $P,z,y$, $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\sum_w\\pi_z(P)_w\\Delta(P;z,w)&\\le5\\epsilon,\n       \\label{ch:mean-discrepancy}\\\\\n \\left|\\sum_w\\pi_z(P)_wH_y(w)-E_r(P^z,y)\\right|\n       &\\le5\\epsilon.\n       \\label{ch:mean-score}\n\\end{aligned}$$ The transfer derivatives satisfy $$\\left(\\sum_w\\frac{|\\dot\\pi_z(P)_w|^2}{\\pi_z(P)_w}\\right)^{1/2}\n       \\le C N_1\\frac{d(h,h')}{r}.\n \\label{ch:string-fisher}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-207.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "anchor": "stmt-ch-string-estimates",
          "exact_quote": "For $M_2$ sufficiently large, uniformly in $P,z,y$, $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\sum_w\\pi_z(P)_w\\Delta(P;z,w)&\\le5\\epsilon,\n       \\label{ch:mean-discrepancy}\\\\\n \\left|\\sum_w\\pi_z(P)_wH_y(w)-E_r(P^z,y)\\right|\n       &\\le5\\epsilon.\n       \\label{ch:mean-score}\n\\end{aligned}$$ The transfer derivatives satisfy $$\\left(\\sum_w\\frac{|\\dot\\pi_z(P)_w|^2}{\\pi_z(P)_w}\\right)^{1/2}\n       \\le C N_1\\frac{d(h,h')}{r}.\n \\label{ch:string-fisher}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-207.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For $M_2$ sufficiently large, uniformly in $P,z,y$, $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\sum_w\\pi_z(P)_w\\Delta(P;z,w)&\\le5\\epsilon,\n       \\label{ch:mean-discrepancy}\\\\\n \\left|\\sum_w\\pi_z(P)_wH_y(w)-E_r(P^z,y)\\right|\n       &\\le5\\epsilon.\n       \\label{ch:mean-score}\n\\end{aligned}$$ The transfer derivatives satisfy $$\\left(\\sum_w\\frac{|\\dot\\pi_z(P)_w|^2}{\\pi_z(P)_w}\\right)^{1/2}\n       \\le C N_1\\frac{d(h,h')}{r}.\n \\label{ch:string-fisher}$$",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-co-regularity",
        "exact_quote": "The forms in <a href=\"#co:forms\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"co:forms\">[co:forms]</a> are well defined, independent of the period cut, and smooth up to every simplex face by one-sided extension. They are invariant under simultaneous translation of all times, under the centralizer action, and under twisted rotation. Their tangential restrictions to faces equal the forms for the shortened tuples, using the same parameters and the same fixed integer $n$.",

      },

      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "hypotheses": [
        {
          "anchor": "co:forms",
          "exact_quote": "Write $\\int_{\\rm per}$ for integration over one period, using a half-open interval when integrating the raw impulse measure, and put $$\\begin{split}\n B_j&=-\\frac12\\int_{\\rm per}du\\int_{\\mathbb R}dv\\,\n             U_j(v-u)K_j(dY_j(u),Y_j(v)),\\\\\n B&=\\sum_{j=0}^{b_*-1}B_j+WB_{b_*}.\n \\end{split}\n \\label{co:forms}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-207.1"
        },
        {
          "anchor": "co:twist-assumption",
          "exact_quote": "We shall construct the connection under either of the assumptions $$\\begin{array}{ll}\n \\textnormal{(i)}&g\\text{ has infinite order},\\\\[2pt]\n \\textnormal{(ii)}&g\\text{ has finite order and }\n                \\displaystyle\\min_{h\\in G}d(h,gh)>4r.\n \\end{array}\n \\label{co:twist-assumption}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-207.1"
        }

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-co-contraction-lemma",
        "exact_quote": "For every stage $0\\leq j\\leq b_*$, $$C_j:=B_j(V)\n =\\int_{\\rm per}\\left(\n K_j(Y_j(u),Y_j(u))\n -\\int_{\\mathbb R}K_j(Y_j(u),Y_j(u+s))\\,d\\rho_j(s)\\right)du.\n \\label{co:contraction}$$ For $j<b_*$, the second integrated pairing is also $$\\int_{\\rm per}\n K_j\\bigl((\\mu_{j+1}*Y_j)(u),(\\mu_{j+1}*Y_j)(u)\\bigr)\\,du.\n \\label{co:averaged-pairing}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-207.1"
      },

          "source_id": "PM-207.1"

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-co-positivity",
        "exact_quote": "Under <a href=\"#co:twist-assumption\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"co:twist-assumption\">[co:twist-assumption]</a>, the scalar function $$C_{\\rm nor}:=B(V)=\\sum_{j=0}^{b_*-1}C_j+WC_{b_*}\n \\label{co:normalizer}$$ satisfies $C_{\\rm nor}\\geq c_0$, where $c_0=1/10$ is independent of all tuples, $n$, $R$, and $g$.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-co-connection",
        "exact_quote": "Let $R$ be sufficiently large, choose the parameters in <a href=\"#co:ladder\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"co:ladder\">[co:ladder]</a> and <a href=\"#co:last-scale\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"co:last-scale\">[co:last-scale]</a>, and let $n>10\\beta_{b_*}$ be even, with time scales <a href=\"#co:time-scales\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"co:time-scales\">[co:time-scales]</a>. Suppose that $g$ satisfies <a href=\"#co:twist-assumption\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"co:twist-assumption\">[co:twist-assumption]</a>. The forms $B_j$ and $B$ in <a href=\"#co:forms\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"co:forms\">[co:forms]</a>, constructed in all dimensions $0\\leq k\\leq n$ with the same parameters, have the following properties. For every smooth stage $1\\leq j\\leq b_*$ and every coordinate $t_i$, $$\\left(\\int_{\\rm per}\\sum_\\omega\n        \\frac{|\\partial_iY_j(u)_\\omega|^2}{Y_j(u)_\\omega}\\,du\n \\right)^{1/2}\n \\leq\\frac{a_jl_i}{\\sqrt{\\delta_j}}.\n \\label{co:fisher}$$ Terms with zero denominator are defined to be zero. Moreover, $$|B(\\partial_i)|+|\\partial_iC_{\\rm nor}|\\leq C_Rl_i.\n \\label{co:coefficients}$$ The scalar $C_{\\rm nor}$ is at least $c_0=1/10$, and $$\\alpha=\\frac{B}{C_{\\rm nor}}\n \\label{co:normalized}$$ is a compatible connection family in the sense of Definition <a href=\"#cy:connection\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"cy:connection\">5</a>. Its coefficients and those of its exterior derivative are bounded by polynomials in the lengths, uniformly over the tuples. In particular, the family may be used in Proposition <a href=\"#cy:trace-identity\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"cy:trace-identity\">6</a>.",
        "source_id": "PM-207.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

          "exact_quote": "Let $R$ be sufficiently large, choose the parameters in <a href=\"#co:ladder\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"co:ladder\">[co:ladder]</a> and <a href=\"#co:last-scale\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"co:last-scale\">[co:last-scale]</a>, and let $n>10\\beta_{b_*}$ be even, with time scales <a href=\"#co:time-scales\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"co:time-scales\">[co:time-scales]</a>. Moreover, $$|B(\\partial_i)|+|\\partial_iC_{\\rm nor}|\\leq C_Rl_i.\n \\label{co:coefficients}$$ The scalar $C_{\\rm nor}$ is at least $c_0=1/10$, and $$\\alpha=\\frac{B}{C_{\\rm nor}}\n \\label{co:normalized}$$ is a compatible connection family in the sense of Definition <a href=\"#cy:connection\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"cy:connection\">5</a>. Its coefficients and those of its exterior derivative are bounded by polynomials in the lengths, uniformly over the tuples. In particular, the family may be used in Proposition <a href=\"#cy:trace-identity\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"cy:trace-identity\">6</a>.",

Its coefficients and those of its exterior derivative are bounded by polynomials in the lengths, uniformly over the tuples. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Compatible connection family** ([source](#stmt-cy-connection)): A compatible connection family through dimension $n$ assigns a real one-form $\alpha_h$ to each $h\in\mathcal T_k(g)$, $0\le k\le n$, on $\widetilde\Delta_k$, with the following properties: 1. It is invariant under time translation, and $\alpha_h(V)=1$. 2. It is invariant under simultaneous left multiplication by $H$ and under twisted rotation of both labels and times. 3. Its tangential restriction to $x_i=0$ agrees with the form of the shortened tuple obtained by deleting $(h_i,t_i)$. 4. The form is smooth up to every face, by restriction of a smooth extension, and the coefficients of $\alpha_h$ and $d\alpha_h$ are bounded by polynomials in the edge lengths, for each fixed dimension and fixed construction parameters.
- **Channel** ([source](#stmt-ch-channel-definition)): A *channel* with label set $\Lambda$, on which $G$ acts, assigns to each $P\in\mathcal P_f(G)$ a finitely supported subprobability $Y(P)=(Y(P)_\omega)_{\omega\in\Lambda}$. Thus $Y(P)_\omega\ge0$ and $\sum_\omega Y(P)_\omega\le1$. Translation of weights means $(sP)_h=P_{s^{-1}h}$ and $(sY)_\omega=Y_{s^{-1}\omega}$. We require left equivariance, $Y(sP)=sY(P)$ for $s\in G$, and the following smoothness property. For each finite $S\subset G$, the restriction to probabilities supported in $S$ has its labels in a fixed finite subset of $\Lambda$, and its coordinate functions extend smoothly to a neighborhood of that probability simplex in $\mathbb R^S$.
- **Centralizer notation** ([source](#pm-h-7)): Fix $g\in G$ and write $H=C_G(g)$ for its centralizer.
- **Finite-order conjugacy classes** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Write $\mathcal C(G)$ for the set of conjugacy classes, and $\mathcal C_{\rm fin}(G)$ for those consisting of finite-order elements.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-concentration-threshold | threshold a_* of the concentration score | = | $0.80$ | radius r at least one |
| q-accuracy-range | accuracy parameter of the summary channels | ≤ | $10^{-3}$ | positive accuracy |
| q-center-diameter | diameter of the set of centers of score above one half | ≤ | $4r$ | all radii r |
| q-string-length | length b of the accurate sample string | ≤ | $Cr\epsilon^{-4}$ | depends only on r and the accuracy |
| q-ball-growth | size of a word ball | ≤ | $e^{\kappa_G s}$ | radius s at least one |
| q-normalizer-lower | lower bound c_0 for the total contraction | = | $1/10$ | independent of tuples, n, R and g |
| q-scale-ladder | scale parameters beta_j | = | $\beta_j^{3/2}$ | successive stages |
| q-stage-count | number of smooth stages b_* | ~ | $O(1+\log R)$ | large radius R |
| q-order-kernel-error | operator-norm error of the rank-N approximation | ≤ | $\frac{M}{2N}$ | every integer N at least one |
| q-singular-decay | singular value s_nu of a compression of the terminal operator | ≤ | $\frac C\nu$ | every index nu at least one |
| q-neighbor-average | averaged product of fourth roots of neighbor counts | ≤ | $C^k R^{k/4}$ | subset size k up to n |
| q-trace-decay | absolute trace coefficient for a surviving-class candidate | ≤ | $C_RM_p\,(Q_RM_p)^n$ | even n tending to infinity, radius fixed |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-reduction** [claim](#stmt-red-reduction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-reduction): Approximate by finite support, correct analytically to an exact idempotent by a power series, and conjugate back by an invertible element. -> similar idempotent with exponential moment on a finitely generated subgroup
- **step-tracial** [claim](#stmt-red-trace) / [proof](#proof-stmt-red-trace): Absolute convergence and exchanging the two factors. -> continuity and similarity invariance of the trace
- **step-weights** [claim](#stmt-cy-weights-lemma) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cy-weights-lemma): Bijection between tuples and increment lists, then idempotency to delete a vertex. -> weight identities and moment bound
- **step-trace-identity** [claim](#stmt-cy-trace-identity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cy-trace-identity): Transgression form and cyclic Stokes argument, then evaluation at the canonical connection. -> trace as weighted curvature Pfaffian average
- **step-center-geometry** [claim](#stmt-ch-center-geometry) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ch-center-geometry): Intersecting supports of mass above one half and ball counting. -> bounded set of centers
- **step-selector** [claim](#stmt-ch-selector-estimates) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ch-selector-estimates): Exponential weights with smooth cutoffs, normalized square-root derivative estimate. -> selector channels with Fisher bound
- **step-terminal-channel** [claim](#stmt-ch-terminal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ch-terminal): Cauchy–Schwarz on the finite support of the terminal channel. -> squared-mass lower bound
- **step-accurate-string** [claim](#stmt-ch-accurate-string) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ch-accurate-string): Exponential-moment concentration for independent samples and a union bound. -> existence of an accurate string
- **step-string-weights** [claim](#stmt-ch-string-estimates) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ch-string-estimates): Gibbs weights on the smoothed discrepancy, comparison with an accurate string. -> mean score error and Fisher bound
- **step-summary** [claim](#stmt-ch-summary) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ch-string-estimates-2): Combine the selector with the early and later prediction channels. -> summary channels in both versions
- **step-regularity** [claim](#stmt-co-regularity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-co-regularity): Explicit formulas for smoothed fields, cut cancellation and face merging of impulses. -> well-defined smooth compatible forms
- **step-contraction** [claim](#stmt-co-contraction-lemma) / [proof](#proof-stmt-co-contraction-lemma): Differentiate a periodic scalar and use the lag derivative identity. -> contraction formula
- **step-positivity** [claim](#stmt-co-positivity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-co-positivity): Telescoping concentration losses with the raw surplus and terminal multiplier. -> uniform positive normalizer
- **step-connection** [claim](#stmt-co-connection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-co-connection): Smeared impulse derivative, Fisher bound per stage, and normalization. -> compatible connection family with bounds
- **step-order-kernel** [claim](#stmt-pf-order-kernel) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pf-order-kernel): Partition the line into intervals of equal mass and drop diagonal blocks. -> finite-rank approximation
- **step-terminal-singular** [claim](#stmt-pf-terminal-singular) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pf-terminal-singular): Orbit block decomposition and rank-error trade-off, then passage to compressions. -> singular value decay
- **step-occupancy** [claim](#stmt-pf-occupancy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pf-occupancy): Cell occupancy comparison with multinomial counting. -> averaged neighbor-count bound
- **step-pfaffian-bound** [claim](#stmt-pf-bound) / [proof](#pf:partition-expansion): Partition expansion of the Pfaffian with stage estimates and an entropy count. -> averaged curvature bound
- **step-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pf-occupancy-2): Fix the radius uniformly, sum the curvature bound against the weights, let the dimension grow, then transfer back by the reduction. -> finite support of the trace

        }

      "inputs": [
        "summary channels"

      ],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-co-contraction-lemma",
          "exact_quote": "For every stage $0\\leq j\\leq b_*$, $$C_j:=B_j(V)\n =\\int_{\\rm per}\\left(\n K_j(Y_j(u),Y_j(u))\n -\\int_{\\mathbb R}K_j(Y_j(u),Y_j(u+s))\\,d\\rho_j(s)\\right)du.\n \\label{co:contraction}$$ For $j<b_*$, the second integrated pairing is also $$\\int_{\\rm per}\n K_j\\bigl((\\mu_{j+1}*Y_j)(u),(\\mu_{j+1}*Y_j)(u)\\bigr)\\,du.\n \\label{co:averaged-pairing}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-207.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "step-contraction",
      "inputs": [
        "regular forms"
      ],
      "method": "Differentiate a periodic scalar and use the lag derivative identity.",
      "output": "contraction formula",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-co-contraction-lemma"
    },
    {

        "step-summary",
        "step-terminal-channel"
      ],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-co-positivity",
          "exact_quote": "Under <a href=\"#co:twist-assumption\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"co:twist-assumption\">[co:twist-assumption]</a>, the scalar function $$C_{\\rm nor}:=B(V)=\\sum_{j=0}^{b_*-1}C_j+WC_{b_*}\n \\label{co:normalizer}$$ satisfies $C_{\\rm nor}\\geq c_0$, where $c_0=1/10$ is independent of all tuples, $n$, $R$, and $g$.",
          "source_id": "PM-207.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "step-positivity",
      "inputs": [
        "contraction formula",
        "summary channels",
        "terminal channel"
      ],
      "method": "Telescoping concentration losses with the raw surplus and terminal multiplier.",
      "output": "uniform positive normalizer",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-co-positivity"
    },
    {
      "claim_anchor": "stmt-co-connection",
      "depends_on": [
        "step-positivity",
        "step-regularity"
      ],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-co-connection",
          "exact_quote": "Let $R$ be sufficiently large, choose the parameters in <a href=\"#co:ladder\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"co:ladder\">[co:ladder]</a> and <a href=\"#co:last-scale\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"co:last-scale\">[co:last-scale]</a>, and let $n>10\\beta_{b_*}$ be even, with time scales <a href=\"#co:time-scales\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"co:time-scales\">[co:time-scales]</a>.",
          "source_id": "PM-207.1"
        }
      ],

          "source_id": "PM-207.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "step-order-kernel",
      "inputs": [
        "nonatomic measures"
      ],
      "method": "Partition the line into intervals of equal mass and drop diagonal blocks.",
      "output": "finite-rank approximation",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-pf-order-kernel"
    },
    {

      ],

        {

          "source_id": "PM-207.1"
        }

    },
    {

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-finite-support**: For an idempotent matrix over the complex l^1 group algebra of a discrete group, there is a finite set of finite-order conjugacy classes outside which every trace coefficient is zero.
- **inv-traciality**: Each conjugacy-class trace functional is continuous and satisfies T_C(ab)=T_C(ba), hence is invariant under similarity.
- **inv-similar-representative**: An exact l^1 idempotent is similar to an idempotent with exponential decay over a finitely generated subgroup, and trace coefficients are preserved.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-finite-support-matrix**: Assume the idempotent matrix or its similar representative has finite support on the group. -> Only the trace functional on conjugacy classes is shown to have finite support; matrix entries may spread over arbitrarily long group elements.
- **anti-nondiscrete-group**: Apply the theorem to continuous or locally compact groups. -> The conjugacy-class indexing and the discrete tuple configurations used in the proof are built from a discrete group.
- **anti-approximate-idempotent**: Apply the conclusion to a matrix that is only approximately idempotent. -> The reduction and the trace identity use exact idempotency; an approximate idempotent carries no such guarantee.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main result is an existence and vanishing statement over arbitrary, possibly infinite, discrete groups; the proof uses limits as the simplex dimension tends to infinity and non-effective constants, so no finite procedure computes the claimed objects.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper proves an existence and finite-support statement about traces of idempotents over a Banach group algebra. It gives no procedure that software could execute on input data. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No dynamics, physical model or stochastic process is simulated. Probability distributions on a group are used only as analytic devices inside the proof. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result gives no checker or library routine. It is a theorem about every discrete group, including groups with no finite description, so no general-purpose tool can verify it. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The argument is non-constructive. It uses an analytic limit as the simplex dimension tends to infinity and gives no finite procedure. The radius cutoff is a proof device, not a computable search bound. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No data structure is proposed. The tuple spaces and simplices are mathematical index objects used in the proof, and the paper gives no storage or access operations for them. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No numerical kernel, fixed-precision arithmetic or parallel computation appears. The estimates are asymptotic inequalities with unspecified constants. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no hardness assumption, protocol or adversary model. The paper treats trace support in K-theory, which has no security reading. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The probability summaries and Fisher seminorms are analytic tools for bounding derivatives of channels on group elements. There is no learning problem, dataset or training objective, and the sampling lemma is an existence argument. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No objective is optimised and no feasibility problem is posed. The maximum over centers is a score used in estimates, not a decision problem. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper involves nodes, messages, consistency or fault tolerance. The cyclic twisted tuples are algebraic objects, not communication structures. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The value is theoretical: it settles the l^1-Bass conjecture for all discrete groups. It has no direct engineering construction, so any use would be as background for formal or K-theoretic work. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-exact-idempotent** (hypothesis): The reduction and the trace identity use exact idempotency in the matrix l^1 algebra. An approximate idempotent is not covered until it is first corrected to an exact one. Blocked use: ['Applying the finite-support conclusion to a numerically or approximately idempotent matrix']
- **limit-discrete-group** (hypothesis): The group is discrete. The conjugacy-class indexing and the cyclic tuple configurations are built from a discrete group. Blocked use: ['Transferring the theorem to Lie groups or locally compact groups with Haar measure']
- **limit-no-finite-support-matrix** (edge_case): The reduction gives exponential decay of the representative, not finite support of its entries. Only the trace functional on conjugacy classes is shown to have finite support. Blocked use: ['Assuming the idempotent or its similar representative is a finite sum of group elements']
- **limit-finite-order-survivors** (formal_scope): The theorem gives a finite set of finite-order classes outside which the coefficients vanish. It does not assert vanishing on every non-trivial finite-order class, and it does not list the surviving classes. Blocked use: ['Concluding that the trace vanishes on all non-trivial finite-order classes', 'Using the theorem as an enumeration of the surviving classes']
- **limit-radius-cutoff-proof-device** (nonconstructive): The finite ball containing the surviving classes comes from a radius fixed once and for all by an analytic limit in the simplex dimension, with constants that are not made explicit. It is a proof device, not a computed bound. Blocked use: ['Using the ball radius as a termination bound in a search over conjugacy classes']
- **limit-large-radius-regime** (hypothesis): The curvature bound holds only for a sufficiently large radius parameter, with constants that depend on the word metric and are not made explicit. Blocked use: ['Reading the Pfaffian bound as valid for small radius', 'Extracting numerical decay rates from the stated estimate']
- **limit-uniform-constants** (approximation): The constants in the averaged bound are independent of the dimension, the twist and the tuple, but they are unspecified. The bound is asymptotic. Blocked use: ['Using the bound for a fixed finite dimension as a quantitative error guarantee']
- **limit-ball-growth-finitely-generated** (formal_scope): The spatial estimates are carried out in a finitely generated subgroup with a fixed word metric and an exponential ball-growth bound. The passage to the whole group is made only by the reduction. Blocked use: ['Applying the channel and curvature estimates directly in a group that is not finitely generated']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the <i>ℓ</i><sup>1</sup>-Bass conjecture for every discrete group. The Hattori–Stallings trace of every idempotent matrix over the complex <i>ℓ</i><sup>1</sup> group algebra is supported on finitely many conjugacy classes of finite-order elements.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Let $G$ be a discrete group. Its complex Banach group algebra $\ell^1(G)$ consists of functions $a\colon G\to\mathbb C$ with $\|a\|_1=\sum_{g\in G}|a(g)|<\infty$, equipped with convolution $$(a*b)(g)=\sum_{h\in G}a(h)b(h^{-1}g).$$ Write $\mathcal C(G)$ for the set of conjugacy classes, and $\mathcal C_{\rm fin}(G)$ for those consisting of finite-order elements. The subscript refers to element order, not to the size of a conjugacy class.

For an idempotent matrix $e=(e_{ij})\in M_q(\ell^1(G))$, its Hattori–Stallings trace (Hattori 1965; Stallings 1965) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Hattori1965](#ref-Hattori1965), [Stallings1965](#ref-Stallings1965)] has coefficients <a id="intro:trace"></a>$$\label{intro:trace}
 \tau_C(e)=\sum_{g\in C}\sum_{i=1}^q e_{ii}(g),
 \qquad C\in\mathcal C(G).$$ These sums converge absolutely, and $\sum_C|\tau_C(e)|\le\sum_i\|e_{ii}\|_1$. The trace is additive under direct sums and depends only on the isomorphism class of the projective module $e\ell^1(G)^q$. It therefore defines $$\operatorname{HS}^1\colon K_0(\ell^1(G))\longrightarrow
                           \ell^1(\mathcal C(G)),$$ where $K_0$ is the Grothendieck group of finitely generated projective right modules. The $\ell^1$-Bass conjecture (Berrick, Chatterji, and Mislin 2004, Conjecture 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[BCM2004](#ref-BCM2004)] asserts that this map takes values in the algebraic direct sum over $\mathcal C_{\rm fin}(G)$. We prove this assertion for all discrete groups.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Let $G$ be a discrete group, let $q\ge1$, and let $e\in M_q(\ell^1(G))$ be idempotent. There is a finite set $F\subset\mathcal C_{\rm fin}(G)$ such that $\tau_C(e)=0$ for every $C\notin F$. Equivalently, $$\operatorname{HS}^1\bigl(K_0(\ell^1(G))\bigr)
 \subseteq \bigoplus_{C\in\mathcal C_{\rm fin}(G)}\mathbb C[C].$$*

</div>

The finite-support assertion is part of the conclusion, in addition to vanishing on infinite-order classes. It is not an automatic consequence of absolute summability. In particular, the proof must make its geometric cutoff independent of the conjugacy class under consideration.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### History and relation to earlier methods

The Hattori–Stallings trace refines the rank of a projective module by taking values in the quotient of its ring by additive commutators (Hattori 1965; Stallings 1965) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Hattori1965](#ref-Hattori1965), [Stallings1965](#ref-Stallings1965)]. For the algebraic group rings $\mathbb ZG$ and $\mathbb CG$, this quotient is indexed by conjugacy classes. Bass’s conjecture for the integral group ring asks that this trace be supported at the identity (Bass 1976) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Bass1976](#ref-Bass1976)]; the complex-group-ring formulation permits finite-order classes. These support questions connect the algebraic structure of projective modules with the conjugacy structure of a group.

Cyclic homology gives one route to these questions. Burghelea’s computation separates group-ring cyclic homology into contributions from finite- and infinite-order conjugacy classes (Burghelea 1985) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Burghelea1985](#ref-Burghelea1985)]. Eckmann used this description and the Chern character to prove Bass-type vanishing for several classes of groups (Eckmann 1986) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Eckmann1986](#ref-Eckmann1986)]. Emmanouil extended this method through nilpotence properties of the cyclic periodicity operator (Emmanouil 1998) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Emmanouil1998](#ref-Emmanouil1998)]. Our argument retains cyclic symmetry but works directly with weighted configurations and differential forms.

Berrick, Chatterji, and Mislin formulated the $\ell^1$-Bass conjecture in the finite-support form used here. They proved it for countable groups whose Bost assembly map is rationally surjective in degree zero (Berrick, Chatterji, and Mislin 2004, Theorem 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BCM2004](#ref-BCM2004)]. Together with Lafforgue’s Banach-algebra $K$-theory (Lafforgue 2002) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Lafforgue2002](#ref-Lafforgue2002)], this includes amenable groups; the passage from countable subgroups to arbitrary groups is explained in (Berrick, Chatterji, and Mislin 2004, Lemma 1.5) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BCM2004](#ref-BCM2004)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> removes the assembly hypothesis while retaining the algebraic finite-support conclusion.

Ji, Ogle, and Ramsey developed related Bass conjectures for rapid-decay completions and higher Chern characters (Ji, Ogle, and Ramsey 2010; Ji, Ogle, and Ramsey 2014) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[JOR2010](#ref-JOR2010), [JOR2014](#ref-JOR2014)]. Their work highlights the growth estimates needed when passing from group rings to analytic completions. Here the summability needed in the proof comes from a similarity reduction to an exponentially decaying idempotent. The subsequent estimates use a spatial scale uniform over all classes to be excluded.

The weighted cyclic configurations and Pfaffian detection strategy build on the complex-group-ring argument in (OpenAI 2026, secs. 7–8) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[OpenAIBass2026](#ref-OpenAIBass2026)]. In the group-ring setting, nonzero products of coefficients involve group elements from a fixed finite set. For an $\ell^1$ idempotent these elements may have arbitrarily large word length, even after reduction to exponential decay. The estimates must retain summable length factors without losing control as the number of factors increases. We obtain them from smooth spatial summaries whose derivative bounds are independent of the size of the input support, multiscale time averaging with a positive translation component, and a final ordered-kernel bound uniform in the closing group element. A single spatial radius then excludes all but finitely many finite-order classes. All ingredients used here are proved below; the complex-group-ring theorem is not an input. Standard concentration and transgression arguments are credited at their points of use.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof strategy

The argument uses Banach algebra power series, differential forms on finite-dimensional simplices, and elementary estimates for integral operators. We give the needed estimates in full.

First, a finite-support approximation followed by an analytic idempotent correction replaces $e$ by a similar idempotent $p$ with an exponential moment in a finitely generated subgroup (Section <a href="#sec:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reduction">2</a>). This step preserves every trace coefficient. It does not assert that $p$ has finite support.

For a fixed $g\in G$, consider cyclic lists of group elements whose closing edge is twisted by multiplication by $g$. Products of the coefficients of $p$ assign complex weights to these lists. Their total weight is $\tau_{[g]}(p)$, and idempotency makes the weights compatible with deleting vertices. The ordered times of a list form a simplex. On these simplices, a one-form invariant under simultaneous time translation and equal to one on the translation vector will be called a connection form. A cyclic Stokes argument shows that the weighted average of its curvature Pfaffian equals the trace coefficient, up to sign (Section <a href="#sec:cyclic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cyclic">3</a>).

The task is then to construct compatible connection forms with small averaged curvature. Interpret a timed list as a step function with values in $G$, and average its Dirac masses on a sequence of time scales. The resulting probability distributions are summarized by smooth subprobability distributions on auxiliary labels. These summaries record spatial concentration while controlling derivatives caused by moving one boundary of the step function (Section <a href="#sec:channels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:channels">4</a>). Pairing their differentials with bounded odd time kernels gives one-forms. Their contractions with time translation telescope across the averaging scales. A final equality kernel detects the twist and leaves a strictly positive contraction, which allows normalization to a connection (Section <a href="#sec:connection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:connection">5</a>).

The curvature estimates use three different features of this construction. At the initial scale, a curvature entry vanishes unless its two times are close; an occupancy estimate controls the resulting sparse Pfaffians on average. At intermediate scales, the short support of the time kernel gives a Hilbert–Schmidt bound. At the last scale, the equality kernel reduces to weighted order kernels, whose singular values satisfy $s_\nu\le C/\nu$. Expanding the Pfaffian across these stages gives exponential decay in the simplex dimension (Section <a href="#sec:curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:curvature">6</a>). A single spatial radius works for every infinite-order class and every finite-order class avoiding a fixed finite word ball. This uniformity proves the finite-support conclusion in Section <a href="#sec:conclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:conclusion">7</a>.

<a id="sec:reduction"></a>
### Reduction to exponential decay

The first step replaces a general $\ell^1$ idempotent by an idempotent whose coefficients have exponential decay in a finitely generated subgroup. The replacement preserves every trace coefficient exactly. This permits the polynomially weighted sums that arise from differential forms in arbitrarily large, but fixed, dimensions.

For a complex matrix $A=(A_{ij})$, write $|A|_1=\sum_{i,j}|A_{ij}|$. On $M_q(\ell^1(G))$ we use $$\|a\|_1=\sum_{h\in G}|a(h)|_1.$$ This norm is submultiplicative. The identity matrix has norm $q$; none of the power-series arguments below requires its norm to be one.

<a id="stmt-red-trace"></a>
<div id="red:trace" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Traciality). *For every conjugacy class $C$ of a discrete group $G$, the functional $$T_C(a)=\sum_{h\in C}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}a(h),\qquad a\in M_q(\ell^1(G)),$$ is continuous and satisfies $T_C(ab)=T_C(ba)$. It is therefore invariant under similarity by invertible matrices over $\ell^1(G)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-trace"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-trace</summary>

*Proof.* The bound $|T_C(a)|\le\|a\|_1$ gives continuity. Expand $T_C(ab)$ as the absolutely convergent sum $$\sum_{\substack{x,y\in G\\xy\in C}}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(a(x)b(y)\bigr).$$ The conditions $xy\in C$ and $yx\in C$ are equivalent, and the scalar matrix trace is cyclic. Interchanging $x$ and $y$ proves the identity. If $u$ is invertible, apply it to $ua$ and $u^{-1}$. ◻

</details>

The same rearrangement applies to rectangular matrices: if $a\in M_{q,r}(\ell^1(G))$ and $b\in M_{r,q}(\ell^1(G))$, then $T_C(ab)=T_C(ba)$, with the trace in the appropriate size on each side. An isomorphism between two finitely generated projective modules, together with its inverse, can be extended by their defining idempotents to such matrices $a,b$ with products equal to the two idempotents. This proves the module invariance used in the definition of $\operatorname{HS}^1$.

<a id="stmt-red-reduction"></a>
<div id="red:reduction" class="proposition">

<a id="red:exponential"></a>
**Proposition 3** (Exponentially decaying representative). *Let $e\in M_q(\ell^1(G))$ satisfy $e^2=e$. There are a finitely generated subgroup $\Gamma\le G$, a word length $|\cdot|$ on $\Gamma$, a number $c>0$, and an idempotent $p\in M_q(\ell^1(\Gamma))$ such that $$\label{red:exponential}
 \sum_{h\in\Gamma}|p(h)|_1e^{c|h|}<\infty,$$ and $p$ is similar to $e$ in $M_q(\ell^1(G))$. If the trace of every such $p$ has finite support on finite-order conjugacy classes of $\Gamma$, the same conclusion holds for every $e$ over every discrete group.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-reduction</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a finite-support matrix $a$ close to $e$ and put $b=4(a-a^2)$. We may require $\|b\|_1<1$. Let $$Q=(1-b)^{-1/2}=\sum_{j=0}^\infty
       \frac{\binom{2j}{j}}{4^j}b^j,
 \qquad
 p=\frac12 I+\left(a-\frac12 I\right)Q.$$ The series converges absolutely in the matrix Banach algebra. All its factors commute with $a$, and the scalar power-series identity gives $Q^2(1-b)=I$. Since $(a-I/2)^2=(I-b)/4$, we obtain $(p-I/2)^2=I/4$, hence $p^2=p$. Moreover $p\to e$ as $a\to e$.

Let $\Gamma$ be generated by the finite union of the supports of the entries of $a$. Fix a finite symmetric generating set and its word length. The norm $$\|v\|_c=\sum_{h\in\Gamma}|v(h)|_1e^{c|h|}$$ is submultiplicative by $|xy|\le |x|+|y|$. The element $b$ has finite support, so $\|b\|_c\to\|b\|_1$ as $c\downarrow0$. Choose $c>0$ with $\|b\|_c<1$. The same series defining $Q$ then converges in this weighted algebra, proving <a href="#red:exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:exponential">[red:exponential]</a>.

Finally, set $$u=pe+(1-p)(1-e).$$ Direct multiplication gives $ue=pu$ and $u-I=(p-e)(2e-I)$. For $a$ sufficiently close to $e$, the latter has norm less than one, so $u$ is invertible and $p=ueu^{-1}$. Lemma <a href="#red:trace" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:trace">2</a> preserves all the trace coefficients.

For a conjugacy class $C$ of $G$, its intersection with $\Gamma$ is a union of conjugacy classes of $\Gamma$. Absolute convergence gives $$T_C(p)=\sum_{\substack{D\in\mathcal C(\Gamma)\\D\subset C}}T_D(p).$$ Thus passing from $\Gamma$ to $G$ merges classes and cannot enlarge a finite set of surviving classes. The order of an element does not change under subgroup inclusion. This proves the final assertion. ◻

</details>

Henceforth $G$ is finitely generated, $d(x,y)=|x^{-1}y|$ is a fixed left-invariant word metric, and $p$ satisfies <a href="#red:exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:exponential">[red:exponential]</a>. We fix a constant $\kappa_G$ such that <a id="red:balls"></a>$$\label{red:balls}
 |B(x,s)|\le e^{\kappa_G s}\qquad(x\in G,\ s\ge1).$$ Such a bound follows by counting words in the generators. It imposes no additional growth hypothesis on $G$. Constants without a subscript may depend on this metric and on fixed construction choices. A constant denoted $C_R$ may also depend on the radius parameter $R$ introduced later, but will not depend on a tuple, its twist $g$, or the dimension $n$.

<a id="sec:cyclic"></a>
### Cyclic simplex formulas for the trace

We now express a conjugacy-class trace coefficient as a weighted average of curvature Pfaffians. Idempotency provides compatibility of the weights under deletion of a vertex; cyclicity supplies the cancellation that makes the average independent of the connection form. The tuple weights have the same algebraic origin as the weighted paths in (OpenAI 2026, sec. 7) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[OpenAIBass2026](#ref-OpenAIBass2026)]; we give the identities needed here directly.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Tuples and their weights

Fix $g\in G$ and write $H=C_G(g)$ for its centralizer. For $k\ge0$ let $$\mathcal T_k(g)=H\backslash G^{k+1},$$ where $H$ acts by simultaneous left multiplication. For a tuple $h=(h_0,\ldots,h_k)$ extend its indices to all integers by $h_{i+k+1}=gh_i$ and define <a id="cy:weights"></a>$$\label{cy:weights}
 v_{k+1}(h)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\left(\prod_{i=0}^k p(h_i^{-1}h_{i+1})\right),
 \qquad l_i=1+d(h_{i-1},h_i).$$ The product is in the displayed order. Both expressions descend to $\mathcal T_k(g)$, and $l_i$ is periodic with period $k+1$.

<a id="stmt-cy-weights-lemma"></a>
<div id="cy:weights-lemma" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Weight identities). *The following properties hold.*

1.  *The weights have total sum $\tau_{[g]}(p)$, in every dimension.*

2.  *Twisted rotation $(h_0,\ldots,h_k)\mapsto(h_1,\ldots,h_k,gh_0)$ preserves the weights.*

3.  *For $k\ge1$, summing the weights over all insertions of one entry into a fixed shortened tuple gives its weight in $\mathcal T_{k-1}(g)$.*

<a id="cy:moment"></a>
*All these sums converge absolutely, even after multiplication by any fixed polynomial in the edge lengths. In particular, with $M_p=\sum_{w\in G}|p(w)|_1(1+|w|)$, $$\label{cy:moment}
 \sum_{h\in\mathcal T_k(g)}|v_{k+1}(h)|\prod_{i=0}^k l_i
 \le M_p^{k+1}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cy-weights-lemma"><summary>Proof of stmt-cy-weights-lemma</summary>

*Proof.* Put $w_i=h_i^{-1}h_{i+1}$. Their product is $h_0^{-1}gh_0$. Conversely, any list $(w_0,\ldots,w_k)$ whose product lies in $[g]$ determines a tuple: choose $h_0$ realizing this conjugate and successively put $h_{i+1}=h_iw_i$. Two choices of $h_0$ differ by left multiplication by an element of $H$, and this is the only ambiguity. We therefore have a bijection between $\mathcal T_k(g)$ and such increment lists.

The inequality $|\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(A_0\cdots A_k)|\le\prod_i|A_i|_1$ gives <a href="#cy:moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:moment">[cy:moment]</a> upon dropping the restriction on the product of the increments. The exponential moment gives the same conclusion for any fixed polynomial in their lengths. The unrestricted convolution sum with product in $[g]$ is the trace coefficient of $p^{k+1}=p$, proving <span class="upright">(i)</span>. Rotation is cyclic permutation of the matrix factors, so <span class="upright">(ii)</span> follows from their trace.

To delete an interior entry, sum the two adjacent factors over that entry. Their convolution is $p^2=p$. The other deletions follow by rotation. One may fix a representative of the shortened tuple because its simultaneous left $H$-action has trivial stabilizer. Thus no multiplicity enters the insertion sum, and <span class="upright">(iii)</span> follows. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Compatible forms

For each tuple use the ordered time domain $$\widetilde\Delta_k
 =\{(t_0,\ldots,t_k):t_0\le t_1\le\cdots\le t_k\le t_0+1\}.$$ Extend times by $t_{i+k+1}=t_i+1$, and set $x_i=t_{i+1}-t_i$ for $0\le i\le k$. Thus $x_i\ge0$ and $\sum_i x_i=1$. Simultaneous time translation is generated by $V=\sum_{i=0}^k\partial_{t_i}$. Its quotient is the simplex $$\Delta_k=\{0\le t_1\le\cdots\le t_k\le1\},$$ using the section $t_0=0$, oriented by $dt_1\wedge\cdots\wedge dt_k$. We regard $[t_i,t_{i+1})$ as carrying the label $h_i$. The face $x_i=0$ corresponds to deleting $(h_i,t_i)$: the interval carrying the label $h_i$ has length zero. At the closing face use the periodic lifts to make this same convention.

<a id="stmt-cy-connection"></a>
<div id="cy:connection" class="definition">

**Definition 5**. A compatible connection family through dimension $n$ assigns a real one-form $\alpha_h$ to each $h\in\mathcal T_k(g)$, $0\le k\le n$, on $\widetilde\Delta_k$, with the following properties:

1.  It is invariant under time translation, and $\alpha_h(V)=1$.

2.  It is invariant under simultaneous left multiplication by $H$ and under twisted rotation of both labels and times.

3.  Its tangential restriction to $x_i=0$ agrees with the form of the shortened tuple obtained by deleting $(h_i,t_i)$.

4.  The form is smooth up to every face, by restriction of a smooth extension, and the coefficients of $\alpha_h$ and $d\alpha_h$ are bounded by polynomials in the edge lengths, for each fixed dimension and fixed construction parameters.

</div>

Translation invariance and $\alpha(V)=1$ imply $\iota_Vd\alpha=0$. Thus $d\alpha$ is a form on $\Delta_k$ independent of the choice of translation section. A translation-invariant form whose contraction with $V$ is zero will be called *basic*.

For a real skew-symmetric $2m\times2m$ matrix $A$, our convention is $$\omega=\sum_{i<j}A_{ij}\,dt_i\wedge dt_j
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \omega^m=m!\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}(A)\,dt_1\wedge\cdots\wedge dt_{2m}.$$ In particular $\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}(A)^2=\det A$. This identity follows by reducing a skew form to two-dimensional blocks, with both sides transforming by the square of the determinant of a change of basis.

<a id="stmt-cy-trace-identity"></a>
<div id="cy:trace-identity" class="proposition">

<a id="cy:trace-formula"></a>
**Proposition 6** (Trace as a curvature average). *Let $n=2m>0$ and let $\alpha$ be a compatible connection family through dimension $n$. Write $A_h$ for the matrix of $d\alpha_h$ on the section $t_0=0$. If $\mathbb E_{\Delta_n}$ denotes uniform probability on $\Delta_n$, then $$\label{cy:trace-formula}
 \sum_{h\in\mathcal T_n(g)}v_{n+1}(h)\,
       \mathbb E_{\Delta_n}\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}(A_h)
   =(-1)^m\tau_{[g]}(p).$$ The series converges absolutely.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cy-trace-identity"><summary>Proof of stmt-cy-trace-identity</summary>

*Proof.* First compare two compatible families $\alpha$ and $\alpha'$. Their difference is basic. The following transgression form is the elementary abelian instance of the connection-invariance mechanism in Chern–Weil theory (Chern and Simons 1974, secs. 2–3) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[ChernSimons1974](#ref-ChernSimons1974)]: $$\eta=(\alpha-\alpha')\wedge
       \sum_{j=0}^{m-1}(d\alpha)^j\wedge(d\alpha')^{m-1-j}$$ is basic, has degree $n-1$, and satisfies $d\eta=(d\alpha)^m-(d\alpha')^m$. It respects all the face and rotation identifications. Stokes’ theorem and Lemma <a href="#cy:weights-lemma" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:weights-lemma">4</a><span class="upright">(iii)</span> express the weighted sum of $\int_{\Delta_n}d\eta$ as a signed sum of copies of $$I=\sum_{h\in\mathcal T_{n-1}(g)}v_n(h)
                       \int_{\Delta_{n-1}}\eta_h.$$ Each face identification has a fixed orientation sign, independent of the labels. The polynomial bounds and the exponential moment justify Stokes term by term and all the rearrangements.

On the simplex of dimension $n-1$, rotation cyclically permutes its $n$ gap coordinates. It has orientation sign $(-1)^{n-1}=-1$. It preserves the weights and the forms. Basicness ensures that returning to the section $t_0=0$ has no additional effect on the integral. Consequently $I=-I$, so $I=0$. The weighted integral of $(d\alpha)^m$ is therefore independent of the compatible family.

Use now the particular connection $$\alpha^{\rm can}=\sum_{i=0}^k x_i\,dt_i.$$ Its contraction with $V$ is one, and all other conditions are immediate from the gap coordinates: on a zero gap the corresponding summand vanishes and adjacent time differentials agree tangentially. On $\widetilde\Delta_k$ its curvature is $$d\alpha^{\rm can}=\sum_{i=0}^k dt_{i+1}\wedge dt_i.$$ For $k=n$ in the section $t_0=0$, the terms at either end vanish, leaving the path of adjacent pairs. Its Pfaffian is $(-1)^m$: the only complete pairing uses $(1,2),(3,4),\ldots,(n-1,n)$, each with coefficient $-1$.

<a id="cy:factorials"></a>
Finally, $\Delta_n$ has Euclidean coordinate volume $1/n!$, so $$\label{cy:factorials}
 \frac{n!}{m!}\int_{\Delta_n}(d\alpha_h)^m
 =\mathbb E_{\Delta_n}\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}(A_h).$$ Evaluation at $\alpha^{\rm can}$ and the total-weight identity give <a href="#cy:trace-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:trace-formula">[cy:trace-formula]</a>. ◻

</details>

The proposition reduces trace vanishing to a quantitative construction. We shall choose connections, in arbitrarily large even dimensions, for which the average of the absolute Pfaffian is at most a small exponential factor times $\prod_{i=1}^n l_i$. Equation <a href="#cy:moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:moment">[cy:moment]</a> then permits summation against the tuple weights. The next section constructs the spatial summaries used to obtain these connections.

<a id="sec:channels"></a>
### Smooth summaries at a spatial scale

The connection forms will be built from smooth summaries of probability distributions on the group. A summary must retain information about spatial concentration while changing in a controlled way when mass moves between two group elements. We construct two versions. The first allows the spatial radius to increase by a factor of six and has a derivative bound independent of that radius. The second retains the radius and has a polynomial dependence on it. Their different bounds will be used at different stages of the averaging construction.

<a id="ch:ball-growth"></a>
Throughout this section, $G$ is a finitely generated group with a fixed left invariant word metric $d$. Write $B(y,s)=\{h:d(h,y)\le s\}$. Counting words in a finite symmetric generating set gives a constant $c_d$ such that $$|B(y,s)|\le \exp(c_d s)\qquad(s\ge1).
 \label{ch:ball-growth}$$ Indeed, if the generating set has $m$ elements, the ball is covered by words of length at most $\lfloor s\rfloor$, and their number is at most $(m+1)^{\lfloor s\rfloor+1}$. Increasing $c_d$ also covers the trivial group. Constants denoted by $C$ may depend on this word metric but not on a radius, an accuracy parameter, or the size of an input support.

<a id="ch:concentration"></a>
Let $\mathcal P_f(G)$ be the set of finitely supported probability distributions on $G$. For $r\ge1$ set $$\begin{split}
 \chi_r(s)&=\min\{1,\max\{0,2-s/r\}\},\qquad s\ge0,\\
 E_r(P,y)&=\sum_{h\in G}P_h\chi_r(d(h,y)),\\
 q_r(P)&=\max_{y\in G}E_r(P,y),\qquad
 \phi_r(P)=(q_r(P)-a_*)_+,\qquad a_*=0.80.
 \end{split}
 \label{ch:concentration}$$ The maximum exists: the score vanishes outside the finite $2r$ neighborhood of $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}P$. It lies in $[0,1]$, so $0\le\phi_r\le0.20$. The functions $q_r$ and $\phi_r$ need not be smooth; they will only be used in inequalities.

<a id="stmt-ch-channel-definition"></a>
<div id="ch:channel-definition" class="definition">

**Definition 7**. A *channel* with label set $\Lambda$, on which $G$ acts, assigns to each $P\in\mathcal P_f(G)$ a finitely supported subprobability $Y(P)=(Y(P)_\omega)_{\omega\in\Lambda}$. Thus $Y(P)_\omega\ge0$ and $\sum_\omega Y(P)_\omega\le1$. Translation of weights means $(sP)_h=P_{s^{-1}h}$ and $(sY)_\omega=Y_{s^{-1}\omega}$. We require left equivariance, $Y(sP)=sY(P)$ for $s\in G$, and the following smoothness property. For each finite $S\subset G$, the restriction to probabilities supported in $S$ has its labels in a fixed finite subset of $\Lambda$, and its coordinate functions extend smoothly to a neighborhood of that probability simplex in $\mathbb R^S$.

</div>

<a id="ch:fisher-definition"></a>
In a transfer direction $\dot P=\delta_h-\delta_{h'}$, derivatives are computed after enlarging $S$ to contain $h,h'$. The channels we construct will have compatible formulas on these enlarged spaces, and their derivatives will vanish at zero output coordinates. For such a channel define its Fisher seminorm in this direction by $$\mathcal F_Y(P;h,h')
 =\left(\sum_{\omega:Y(P)_\omega>0}
       \frac{|\dot Y(P)_\omega|^2}{Y(P)_\omega}\right)^{1/2}.
 \label{ch:fisher-definition}$$ The zero-coordinate assertion is part of the construction, and will be proved; it is not a convention for discarding a nonzero derivative.

If $A$ has label set $G$ and $L$ has label set $\Lambda$, a *cross kernel* is a function $\kappa:G\times\Lambda\to[0,1]$ satisfying $\kappa(sy,s\omega)=\kappa(y,\omega)$. It is fixed independently of $P$. It gives the predictions <a id="ch:prediction-definition"></a>$$\operatorname{pred}(P,y)
   =\sum_{\omega\in\Lambda}\kappa(y,\omega)L(P)_\omega.
 \label{ch:prediction-definition}$$ Exact predictions alone would be easy: $L(P)=P$ and $\kappa(y,h)=\chi_r(d(h,y))$ give $\operatorname{pred}(P,y)=E_r(P,y)$. When $G$ has at least two elements, this choice has no uniform Fisher bound: a transfer between distinct elements, adding mass to an entry of size $t>0$, contributes $1/t$ to the squared seminorm, which diverges as $t\downarrow0$. The approximation allowed in the next proposition permits uniform derivative control, including at probability faces.

<a id="stmt-ch-summary"></a>
<div id="ch:summary" class="proposition">

<a id="ch:selection-bound"></a>
<a id="ch:prediction-lower"></a>
<a id="ch:summary-fisher"></a>
**Proposition 8** (Spatial summary channels). *Fix $r\ge1$ and $0<\epsilon\le10^{-3}$. There are channels $A,L$ and a cross kernel $\kappa$ with either of the following two choices of radius: the early version has $r'=6r$, and the later version has $r'=r$. In both versions $A$ is labelled by $G$, and for every $P\in\mathcal P_f(G)$ and $y\in G$, <a id="ch:prediction-upper"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{z\in G}A(P)_z\bigl(E_r(P,z)-a_*\bigr)
       &\ge\phi_r(P)-10\epsilon,
       \label{ch:selection-bound}\\
 \operatorname{pred}(P,y)
       &\le E_{r'}(P,y)+10\epsilon,
       \label{ch:prediction-upper}\\
 \operatorname{pred}(P,y)
       &\ge E_r(P,y)-10\epsilon
       \qquad\text{if }A(P)_y>0.
       \label{ch:prediction-lower}
\end{aligned}$$ For $Y=A$ or $Y=L$, derivatives in every transfer direction vanish at zero coordinates and satisfy $$\mathcal F_Y(P;h,h')\le C(1+d(h,h'))
 \begin{cases}
   \epsilon^{-10},&\text{early version},\\
   r^{10}\epsilon^{-10},&\text{later version}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{ch:summary-fisher}$$ Here $C$ depends only on the fixed word metric. The numerical error constant $10$ in <a href="#ch:selection-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:selection-bound">[ch:selection-bound]</a>–<a href="#ch:prediction-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:prediction-lower">[ch:prediction-lower]</a> is independent of the group and of the parameters.*

</div>

<a id="ch:averaging-drop"></a>
To see why these guarantees are suited to averaging, assume them for the moment. Take a finite family $P^{(\nu)}$ of input probabilities and weights $\theta_\nu\ge0$ with $\sum_\nu\theta_\nu=1$, and write $$\overline P=\sum_\nu\theta_\nu P^{(\nu)},\qquad
 \overline A_y=\sum_\nu\theta_\nu A(P^{(\nu)})_y,\qquad
 \overline{\operatorname{pred}}_y
       =\sum_\nu\theta_\nu\operatorname{pred}(P^{(\nu)},y).$$ The selection and lower prediction bounds give $\sum_yA(P)_y(\operatorname{pred}(P,y)-a_*)\ge\phi_r(P)-20\epsilon$. On the other hand, the upper prediction bound and linearity of $E_{r'}$ give $\overline{\operatorname{pred}}_y
\le E_{r'}(\overline P,y)+10\epsilon$. Since $\overline A$ is a subprobability, its pairing with $E_{r'}(\overline P,\cdot)-a_*$ is at most $\phi_{r'}(\overline P)$; if the maximal centered score is negative, that pairing is nonpositive. Finally, $\sum_\nu\theta_\nu\sum_yA(P^{(\nu)})_y=\sum_y\overline A_y$, so the terms containing $a_*$ cancel and $$\begin{split}
 &\sum_\nu\theta_\nu\sum_y
       A(P^{(\nu)})_y\operatorname{pred}(P^{(\nu)},y)
       -\sum_y\overline A_y\overline{\operatorname{pred}}_y\\
 &\hspace{2em}\ge
       \sum_\nu\theta_\nu\phi_r(P^{(\nu)})
       -\phi_{r'}(\overline P)-30\epsilon.
 \end{split}
 \label{ch:averaging-drop}$$ Thus loss of concentration under averaging gives a lower bound for the difference of the two pairings. The same calculation applies to time averages of fields with a common finite support, which is how these estimates will enter the connection construction.

We first construct $A$ and a second distribution $D$ on possible centers. The channel $A$ concentrates near a maximizing center when the maximal score exceeds $a_*$. The thresholds for $D$ are lower, so that $D$ already has almost full mass whenever $A$ is nonzero. The two versions of $L$ will then use this same $D$ to predict scores at the selected centers.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Smooth selection of centers

The number of centers that can carry substantial score has a bound independent of $|\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}P|$.

<a id="stmt-ch-center-geometry"></a>
<div id="ch:center-geometry" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9**. *For $P\in\mathcal P_f(G)$, the set $\{y:E_r(P,y)>1/2\}$ has diameter at most $4r$ and cardinality at most $\exp(\gamma r)$, where $\gamma$ depends only on the word metric.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ch-center-geometry"><summary>Proof of stmt-ch-center-geometry</summary>

*Proof.* The inequality $E_r(P,y)>1/2$ implies $P(\{h:d(h,y)<2r\})>1/2$. Any two such sets intersect, since their masses add to more than one. Their centers consequently have distance less than $4r$. If the set of centers is nonempty, choose one of them. All the others lie in its $4r$ ball, and <a href="#ch:ball-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:ball-growth">[ch:ball-growth]</a> gives the claimed bound, for example with $\gamma=4c_d$. The empty case is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="ch:cutoffs"></a>
Choose smooth functions $\psi_A,\psi_D:\mathbb R\to[0,1]$ such that $$\begin{array}{c|cc}
       &\text{identically zero on}&\text{identically one on}\\ \hline
 \psi_A&(-\infty,0.70]&[0.75,\infty)\\
 \psi_D&(-\infty,0.58]&[0.62,\infty).
 \end{array}
 \label{ch:cutoffs}$$ For completeness, a smooth increasing cutoff from zero to one on $[0,1]$ is $\eta(t)/(\eta(t)+\eta(1-t))$, where $\eta(t)=e^{-1/t}$ for $t>0$ and $\eta(t)=0$ for $t\le0$. Every right derivative of $\eta$ at zero vanishes: away from zero it is $e^{-1/t}$ times a polynomial in $1/t$, which tends to zero as $t\downarrow0$. The denominator in the displayed quotient is everywhere positive, and affine changes of variable give <a href="#ch:cutoffs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:cutoffs">[ch:cutoffs]</a>. Their first derivatives are bounded by a fixed numerical constant, since they vanish outside fixed compact transition intervals.

<a id="ch:selector-formulas"></a>
Take $N=M_0r\epsilon^{-2}$ and define $$\begin{split}
 w^A_y&=\psi_A(E_r(P,y))^2
              e^{N(E_r(P,y)-0.80)},\\
 w^D_y&=\psi_D(E_r(P,y))^2
              e^{N(E_r(P,y)-0.66)},\\
 A(P)_y&=\frac{w^A_y}{1+\sum_z w^A_z},\qquad
 D(P)_y=\frac{w^D_y}{1+\sum_z w^D_z}.
 \end{split}
 \label{ch:selector-formulas}$$ Here $M_0$ is fixed in terms of the metric, large enough that for all allowed $r,\epsilon$, <a id="ch:exponential-choices"></a>$$(1+e^{\gamma r})e^{-N\epsilon}\le\epsilon,
 \qquad e^{\gamma r-0.04N}\le1.
 \label{ch:exponential-choices}$$ For example, any $M_0\ge25\gamma+4$ suffices. Indeed, $\log(1+e^{\gamma r})+\log(1/\epsilon)
\le\gamma r+\log2+1/\epsilon\le(\gamma+2)r/\epsilon$, and $N\ge25\gamma r$.

The additional $1$ in each denominator may be regarded as the weight of an unused null label. It allows the channel to have small total mass when no center reaches the exponential threshold. Each displayed sum is finite by Lemma <a href="#ch:center-geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:center-geometry">9</a>.

<a id="stmt-ch-selector-estimates"></a>
<div id="ch:selector-estimates" class="lemma">

<a id="ch:selector-concentration"></a>
<a id="ch:D-concentration"></a>
<a id="ch:selector-fisher"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Selector estimates). *The formulas <a href="#ch:selector-formulas" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:selector-formulas">[ch:selector-formulas]</a> define smooth equivariant channels. They satisfy $$\sum_y A(P)_y(E_r(P,y)-a_*)\ge\phi_r(P)-3\epsilon.
 \label{ch:selector-concentration}$$ Whenever $q_r(P)>0.70$, $$\sum_zD(P)_z\ge1-\epsilon,
 \qquad
 \sum_zD(P)_zE_r(P,z)\ge q_r(P)-2\epsilon.
