 \label{ch:D-concentration}$$ Both channels have zero derivatives at zero coordinates, and $$\mathcal F_A(P;h,h')+\mathcal F_D(P;h,h')
       \le C\epsilon^{-2}d(h,h').
 \label{ch:selector-fisher}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ch-selector-estimates"><summary>Proof of stmt-ch-selector-estimates</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $P$ and abbreviate $E_r(P,y)$ to $E_y$ and $q_r(P)$ to $q$. Suppose first that $q>a_*+\epsilon$. At a maximizing center the cutoff $\psi_A$ is one, and its weight is $e^{N(q-a_*)}$. The normalized mass of centers with $E_y<q-\epsilon$, together with the null mass, is at most $(1+e^{\gamma r})e^{-N\epsilon}\le\epsilon$. On the remaining centers $E_y-a_*\ge q-a_*-\epsilon$; on all centers $E_y-a_*\ge-1$. Their weighted sum is therefore at least $q-a_*-3\epsilon$. If $q\le a_*+\epsilon$, the total mass at centers with $E_y<a_*-\epsilon$ is at most $e^{\gamma r}e^{-N\epsilon}\le\epsilon$, using the $1$ in the denominator. All other negative contributions are bounded below by $-\epsilon$ times their mass. The sum is at least $-2\epsilon$, whereas $\phi_r(P)\le\epsilon$. This proves <a href="#ch:selector-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:selector-concentration">[ch:selector-concentration]</a> in both cases.

Now assume $q>0.70$. The cutoff $\psi_D$ is one at a maximizing center. The same comparison, with threshold $0.66$, shows that the total mass of the null label and the centers with $E_z<q-\epsilon$ is at most $\epsilon$. Indeed the null-weight ratio is $e^{-N(q-0.66)}\le e^{-N\epsilon}$. Thus the nonnull mass is at least $1-\epsilon$, and the score average is at least $(1-\epsilon)(q-\epsilon)\ge q-2\epsilon$. This proves <a href="#ch:D-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:D-concentration">[ch:D-concentration]</a>.

<a id="ch:score-derivative"></a>
<a id="ch:normalization-derivative"></a>
We next prove the derivative estimate. Put $d_0=d(h,h')$. The function $\chi_r$ is $1/r$-Lipschitz, so $$|\dot E_y|
 =|\chi_r(d(h,y))-\chi_r(d(h',y))|
 \le d_0/r.
 \label{ch:score-derivative}$$ The following calculation applies to either cutoff, with its corresponding threshold $\theta\in\{0.80,0.66\}$. Set $$f_y=\psi(E_y)e^{N(E_y-\theta)/2},\qquad
 F=(1,(f_y)_y),\qquad Z=\|F\|^2=1+\sum_y f_y^2.$$ The nonnull coordinates of $F/\|F\|$ are the smooth nonnegative square roots of the normalized weights. Differentiating a normalized Euclidean vector gives $$\left\|\frac{d}{dt}\frac{F}{\|F\|}\right\|^2
 \le\frac{\|\dot F\|^2}{Z}.
 \label{ch:normalization-derivative}$$ To see this, the derivative is $\|F\|^{-1}$ times the orthogonal projection of $\dot F$ onto $F^\perp$.

<a id="ch:cutoff-derivative-estimate"></a>
Differentiating $f_y$ and using $(u+v)^2\le2u^2+2v^2$ gives $$\frac{\|\dot F\|^2}{Z}
 \le C\frac{d_0^2}{r^2}
 \left(
  \frac{\sum_y|\psi'(E_y)|^2e^{N(E_y-\theta)}}{Z}
   +N^2\frac{\sum_y f_y^2}{Z}
 \right).
 \label{ch:cutoff-derivative-estimate}$$ The second fraction is at most one. A nonzero cutoff derivative requires $E_y\le0.75$ for $A$ or $E_y\le0.62$ for $D$. In either case $E_y-\theta\le-0.04$. Lemma <a href="#ch:center-geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:center-geometry">9</a> bounds the number of these terms by $e^{\gamma r}$. The first fraction is consequently at most $Ce^{\gamma r-0.04N}\le C$. Equations <a href="#ch:normalization-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:normalization-derivative">[ch:normalization-derivative]</a> and <a href="#ch:cutoff-derivative-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:cutoff-derivative-estimate">[ch:cutoff-derivative-estimate]</a> now bound the squared norm of the square-root derivative by $C(1+N^2)d_0^2/r^2$. At a positive normalized weight $Y_y$, $|\dot Y_y|^2/Y_y=4|\frac{d}{dt}\sqrt{Y_y}|^2$. Substituting $N=M_0r\epsilon^{-2}$ proves <a href="#ch:selector-fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:selector-fisher">[ch:selector-fisher]</a>.

These computations also justify regularity at zero coordinates. A smooth nonnegative function has zero derivative wherever it vanishes. Thus $\psi(E_y)=0$ implies $\dot f_y=0$, even in a transfer direction that leaves a probability face. Hence the normalized weight and its square root both have zero derivative there.

Finally, on distributions supported in a fixed finite $S$, only centers within its $2r$ neighborhood can have a nonzero score. The formulas in <a href="#ch:selector-formulas" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:selector-formulas">[ch:selector-formulas]</a> are therefore finite smooth formulas on $\mathbb R^S$, with positive denominators. Enlarging $S$ adds the same formulas for the additional coordinates. This proves the stated smoothness and compatibility at faces. Left equivariance follows from the left invariance of $d$. ◻

</details>

The final averaging stage will only need $D$, together with its concentration on a bounded set of labels. We record that consequence separately.

<a id="stmt-ch-terminal"></a>
<div id="ch:terminal" class="lemma">

<a id="ch:terminal-square-mass"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Terminal channel). *For each $r\ge1$ and $0<\epsilon\le10^{-3}$, the channel $D$ in <a href="#ch:selector-formulas" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:selector-formulas">[ch:selector-formulas]</a> is smooth, left equivariant, and has total mass at most one. Its nonempty support has diameter at most $4r$ and cardinality at most $e^{\gamma r}$, with $\gamma$ depending only on the word metric. If $q_r(P)>0.70$, its mass is at least $1-\epsilon\ge1/2$. Its transfer derivatives vanish at zero coordinates and satisfy $$\mathcal F_D(P;h,h')\le C\epsilon^{-2}d(h,h')
       \le C\epsilon^{-10}(1+d(h,h')).$$ In particular, if $\phi_r(P)>0$, then $$\sum_zD(P)_z^2\ge\frac14e^{-\gamma r}.
 \label{ch:terminal-square-mass}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ch-terminal"><summary>Proof of stmt-ch-terminal</summary>

*Proof.* Every center in the support of $D$ has score greater than $0.58>1/2$. Apply Lemma <a href="#ch:center-geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:center-geometry">9</a> and Lemma <a href="#ch:selector-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:selector-estimates">10</a>. If $\phi_r(P)>0$, then $q_r(P)>0.80$, so $\sum_zD_z\ge1/2$. The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality on the finite support gives $\sum_zD_z^2\ge(\sum_zD_z)^2/|\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}D|$, proving <a href="#ch:terminal-square-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:terminal-square-mass">[ch:terminal-square-mass]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Predictions with an enlarged radius

<a id="ch:early-channel"></a>
The early channel can use the average score of the centers chosen by $D$. All these centers lie near any center selected by $A$. Enlarging the radius allows us to bound each score contributing at $y$ by $E_{6r}(P,y)$. Define $$\Lambda=G,\qquad
 L(P)_z=D(P)_zE_r(P,z),\qquad
 \kappa(y,z)=\mathbf1_{\{d(y,z)\le4r\}}.
 \label{ch:early-channel}$$ This is a subprobability channel, since $0\le E_r(P,z)\le1$. When $d(y,z)\le4r$, every $h$ with $\chi_r(d(h,z))>0$ has $d(h,y)<6r$. On this set $\chi_{6r}(d(h,y))=1$, and therefore <a id="ch:radius-domination"></a>$$E_r(P,z)\le E_{6r}(P,y).
 \label{ch:radius-domination}$$ After summing against $D$, this proves $\operatorname{pred}(P,y)\le E_{6r}(P,y)$.

If $A(P)_y>0$, then $E_r(P,y)>0.70$ and $q_r(P)>0.70$. For every $z$ with $D(P)_z>0$, Lemma <a href="#ch:center-geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:center-geometry">9</a> gives $d(y,z)\le4r$. Hence every such $z$ contributes to the prediction, and <a href="#ch:D-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:D-concentration">[ch:D-concentration]</a> gives $$\operatorname{pred}(P,y)
 =\sum_zD(P)_zE_r(P,z)
 \ge q_r(P)-2\epsilon
 \ge E_r(P,y)-2\epsilon.$$

<a id="ch:early-fisher"></a>
To estimate derivatives, write $E_z=E_r(P,z)$ again. Where $D_z>0$, $E_z>0.58$. Differentiating $L_z=D_zE_z$ yields $$\sum_{z:L_z>0}\frac{|\dot L_z|^2}{L_z}
 \le 2\sum_{z:D_z>0}E_z\frac{|\dot D_z|^2}{D_z}
       +2\sum_{z:D_z>0}D_z\frac{|\dot E_z|^2}{E_z}
 \le C\epsilon^{-4}d(h,h')^2.
 \label{ch:early-fisher}$$ In the second sum only $D_z>0$ is relevant; its denominators are thus bounded below by $0.58$. We used <a href="#ch:score-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:score-derivative">[ch:score-derivative]</a>, <a href="#ch:selector-fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:selector-fisher">[ch:selector-fisher]</a>, and $r\ge1$. At a zero coordinate $D_z=0$ and $\dot D_z=0$, so $\dot L_z=0$. The same finite formulas prove smoothness at faces. This establishes all the early-version claims of Proposition <a href="#ch:summary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:summary">8</a>.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Accurate finite sample strings

To keep the radius equal to $r$, an average of center scores is insufficient: we must predict the score separately at each $y$. A finite string of sample points approximates all scores in a bounded region simultaneously. Random sampling will prove the existence of an accurate string. We will then put smooth positive weights on *all* strings in a fixed finite alphabet. This distinction is useful at probability faces: the defining weights will not be products of possibly vanishing input probabilities.

<a id="ch:truncated-probability"></a>
<a id="ch:truncation-exactness"></a>
Fix a center $z\in G$. Introduce a null symbol $\partial$, fixed by the group action, and the alphabet $$\mathcal A_z=B(z,20r)\cup\{\partial\}.$$ Define a probability $Q^z$ on this alphabet by $$P^z_h=P_h\chi_{10r}(d(h,z)),\qquad
 Q^z_h=P^z_h\ (h\ne\partial),\qquad
 Q^z_\partial=1-\sum_hP^z_h.
 \label{ch:truncated-probability}$$ The notation $E_r(P^z,y)$ has the same linear meaning as in <a href="#ch:concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:concentration">[ch:concentration]</a>, although $P^z$ can have mass less than one. This truncation provides a fixed finite alphabet while preserving every score needed for the lower prediction bound. Indeed, if $A(P)_yD(P)_z>0$, Lemma <a href="#ch:center-geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:center-geometry">9</a> gives $d(y,z)\le4r$. Every $h$ contributing to $E_r(P,y)$ then has $d(h,z)<6r$, where the factor $\chi_{10r}(d(h,z))$ is one. Consequently $$E_r(P^z,y)=E_r(P,y)
 \qquad\text{if }A(P)_yD(P)_z>0.
 \label{ch:truncation-exactness}$$ For arbitrary $y$, we still have $E_r(P^z,y)\le E_r(P,y)$, which is the direction needed for the upper bound. Thus accurate predictions for $P^z$ will suffice. The Lipschitz taper in <a href="#ch:truncated-probability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:truncated-probability">[ch:truncated-probability]</a> will also keep their transfer derivatives proportional to $d(h,h')/r$.

For a positive integer $b$ and a string $w=(w_1,\ldots,w_b)\in\mathcal A_z^b$ let <a id="ch:string-discrepancy"></a>$$H_y(w)=\frac1b\sum_{i:w_i\ne\partial}\chi_r(d(w_i,y)),
 \qquad
 \Delta(P;z,w)=\max_{y\in B(z,22r)}
          |H_y(w)-E_r(P^z,y)|.
 \label{ch:string-discrepancy}$$ Both scores vanish when $d(y,z)>22r$, by the triangle inequality. Thus $\Delta$ also controls the discrepancy at every $y\in G$. Always $0\le\Delta\le1$.

<a id="stmt-ch-accurate-string"></a>
<div id="ch:accurate-string" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12**. *There is a constant $C$, depending only on the word metric, and an integer $b$ depending only on $r,\epsilon$, with $1\le b\le Cr\epsilon^{-4}$, such that for every $P$ and $z$ some $w\in\mathcal A_z^b$ satisfies $\Delta(P;z,w)\le\epsilon$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ch-accurate-string"><summary>Proof of stmt-ch-accurate-string</summary>

*Proof.* Take $b$ independent samples with distribution $Q^z$. For a fixed test center $y$, each summand in $H_y$ lies in $[0,1]$ and has mean $E_r(P^z,y)$. The bounded-variable concentration estimate of Hoeffding (Hoeffding 1963) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Hoeffding1963](#ref-Hoeffding1963)] gives the needed probability bound. We include a direct exponential-moment proof, with sufficient constants. If a real random variable $X$ has mean zero and $|X|\le1$, then, for $|t|\le1$, $$\mathbf E e^{tX}
 \le1+\sum_{k\ge2}\frac{|t|^k}{k!}
 \le1+t^2\le e^{t^2}.$$ For independent copies $X_1,\ldots,X_b$, Markov’s inequality and independence, with $t=\epsilon/2$, imply $$\mathbf P\!\left(\frac1b\sum_iX_i>\epsilon\right)
 \le e^{-tb\epsilon}\prod_i\mathbf E e^{tX_i}
 \le e^{-b\epsilon^2/4}.$$ Applying the same argument to $-X_i$ gives the bound $2e^{-b\epsilon^2/4}$ for absolute error exceeding $\epsilon$.

By <a href="#ch:ball-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:ball-growth">[ch:ball-growth]</a>, there is a fixed $c_1$ such that the number of test centers is at most $e^{c_1r}$. A union bound therefore gives $$\mathbf P\bigl(\Delta(P;z,w)>\epsilon\bigr)
 \le2e^{c_1r-b\epsilon^2/4}.$$ Choose $b=\lceil M_1r\epsilon^{-4}\rceil$ with a sufficiently large fixed $M_1$. The right side is strictly less than one for all permitted $r,\epsilon$. At least one string is accurate. Since $r\epsilon^{-4}\ge1$, rounding preserves the asserted upper bound on $b$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Smooth weights on strings

<a id="ch:smooth-discrepancy"></a>
<a id="ch:smoothing-error"></a>
Fix the integer $b$ supplied by Lemma <a href="#ch:accurate-string" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:accurate-string">12</a>. To assign smooth weights, replace the maximum of the signed errors by the logarithm of a sum of exponentials. Put $$S(P;z,w)=\frac1\lambda\log
 \left(\sum_{y\in B(z,22r)}\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}
   \exp\bigl(\lambda\sigma(H_y(w)-E_r(P^z,y))\bigr)\right).
 \label{ch:smooth-discrepancy}$$ Choose $\lambda>0$, depending only on $r,\epsilon$, so large that the logarithm of the number of signed tests is at most $\lambda\epsilon$. The number of tests is independent of $z$, since left translation identifies the balls. The elementary inequalities $e^{\lambda\max x_i}\le\sum_i e^{\lambda x_i}
\le M e^{\lambda\max x_i}$ give $$\Delta(P;z,w)\le S(P;z,w)\le\Delta(P;z,w)+\epsilon.
 \label{ch:smoothing-error}$$

<a id="ch:smooth-discrepancy-derivative"></a>
The first derivative of this smoothed maximum has a particularly simple bound. The test function $h\mapsto\chi_{10r}(d(h,z))\chi_r(d(h,y))$ is $11/(10r)$-Lipschitz: both factors have absolute value at most one, and their Lipschitz constants add. Thus every signed error in <a href="#ch:smooth-discrepancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:smooth-discrepancy">[ch:smooth-discrepancy]</a> has transfer derivative of absolute value at most $11d(h,h')/(10r)$. Differentiation of the logarithm gives a convex combination of these derivatives; the factor $\lambda$ cancels. Consequently $$|\dot S(P;z,w)|\le\frac{11}{10r}d(h,h').
 \label{ch:smooth-discrepancy-derivative}$$ In particular this estimate does not grow with the number of tests or the smoothing parameter.

<a id="ch:gibbs-strings"></a>
Now define a probability distribution on the entire fixed set of strings by $$\pi_z(P)_w
 =\frac{e^{-N_1S(P;z,w)}}{\sum_{v\in\mathcal A_z^b}e^{-N_1S(P;z,v)}},
 \qquad N_1=M_2rb\epsilon^{-2}.
 \label{ch:gibbs-strings}$$ Every coordinate is strictly positive. The constant $M_2$ will be fixed in terms of the metric.

<a id="stmt-ch-string-estimates"></a>
<div id="ch:string-estimates" class="lemma">

<a id="ch:mean-discrepancy"></a>
<a id="ch:mean-score"></a>
<a id="ch:string-fisher"></a>
**Lemma 13**. *For $M_2$ sufficiently large, uniformly in $P,z,y$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_w\pi_z(P)_w\Delta(P;z,w)&\le5\epsilon,
       \label{ch:mean-discrepancy}\\
 \left|\sum_w\pi_z(P)_wH_y(w)-E_r(P^z,y)\right|
       &\le5\epsilon.
       \label{ch:mean-score}
\end{aligned}$$ The transfer derivatives satisfy $$\left(\sum_w\frac{|\dot\pi_z(P)_w|^2}{\pi_z(P)_w}\right)^{1/2}
       \le C N_1\frac{d(h,h')}{r}.
 \label{ch:string-fisher}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ch-string-estimates"><summary>Proof of stmt-ch-string-estimates</summary>

*Proof.* There are at most $e^{c_2rb}$ strings, for a constant $c_2$ fixed by the metric. By Lemma <a href="#ch:accurate-string" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:accurate-string">12</a> and <a href="#ch:smoothing-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:smoothing-error">[ch:smoothing-error]</a>, at least one string has $S\le2\epsilon$. Every string with $\Delta>4\epsilon$ has $S>4\epsilon$, and hence a weight at most $e^{-2N_1\epsilon}$ times the weight of that particular string. Therefore $$\sum_{w:\Delta(P;z,w)>4\epsilon}\pi_z(P)_w
 \le e^{c_2rb-2N_1\epsilon}\le\epsilon$$ once $M_2$ is sufficiently large. One fixed choice works for all $r\ge1$, $b\ge1$, and $\epsilon\le10^{-3}$, as is seen by taking logarithms and using $\log(1/\epsilon)\le1/\epsilon$. Since $\Delta\le1$, splitting the expectation at $4\epsilon$ proves <a href="#ch:mean-discrepancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:mean-discrepancy">[ch:mean-discrepancy]</a>. The discrepancy controls each test center, and both scores vanish outside the test ball, proving <a href="#ch:mean-score" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:mean-score">[ch:mean-score]</a> for every $y$.

Differentiating the normalized exponential in <a href="#ch:gibbs-strings" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:gibbs-strings">[ch:gibbs-strings]</a> gives $$\frac{\dot\pi_z(P)_w}{\pi_z(P)_w}
 =-N_1\left(\dot S(P;z,w)
       -\sum_v\pi_z(P)_v\dot S(P;z,v)\right).$$ Equation <a href="#ch:smooth-discrepancy-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:smooth-discrepancy-derivative">[ch:smooth-discrepancy-derivative]</a> bounds its absolute value by $C N_1d(h,h')/r$. Squaring and summing against the probability $\pi_z(P)$ proves <a href="#ch:string-fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:string-fisher">[ch:string-fisher]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Predictions at the original radius

<a id="ch:later-channel"></a>
We can now finish the later channel. Its label set consists of pairs $(z,w)$, with $z\in G$ and $w\in\mathcal A_z^b$. Translation acts on the center and on every nonnull entry of the string. Define $$L(P)_{z,w}=D(P)_z\pi_z(P)_w,
 \qquad \kappa(y,(z,w))=H_y(w).
 \label{ch:later-channel}$$ The total mass of $L$ is $\sum_zD(P)_z\le1$, and the kernel takes values in $[0,1]$. All formulas commute with left translation.

By <a href="#ch:mean-score" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:mean-score">[ch:mean-score]</a>, and since $P^z_h\le P_h$, $$\operatorname{pred}(P,y)
 \le\sum_zD(P)_z\bigl(E_r(P^z,y)+5\epsilon\bigr)
 \le E_r(P,y)+5\epsilon.$$ Suppose next that $A(P)_y>0$. For every $z$ with $D(P)_z>0$, <a href="#ch:truncation-exactness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:truncation-exactness">[ch:truncation-exactness]</a> identifies the predicted truncated score with $E_r(P,y)$. Moreover $q_r(P)>0.70$, and <a href="#ch:D-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:D-concentration">[ch:D-concentration]</a> gives $\sum_zD(P)_z\ge1-\epsilon$. It follows that $$\operatorname{pred}(P,y)
 \ge E_r(P,y)\sum_zD(P)_z-5\epsilon\sum_zD(P)_z
 \ge E_r(P,y)-6\epsilon.$$ These are the required prediction bounds with $r'=r$.

<a id="ch:later-fisher"></a>
For the derivative bound it is useful to retain the product structure $L_{z,w}=D_z\pi_z(w)$. Since $\sum_w\pi_z(w)=1$ and $\sum_w\dot\pi_z(w)=0$, expansion of the square gives the exact identity <a id="ch:product-fisher"></a>$$\sum_{z,w:L_{z,w}>0}\frac{|\dot L_{z,w}|^2}{L_{z,w}}
 =\sum_{z:D_z>0}\frac{|\dot D_z|^2}{D_z}
   +\sum_zD_z\sum_w\frac{|\dot\pi_z(w)|^2}{\pi_z(w)}.
 \label{ch:product-fisher}$$ All weights and derivatives here are real; the mixed term vanishes by the displayed zero-sum identity. Using <a href="#ch:selector-fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:selector-fisher">[ch:selector-fisher]</a>, <a href="#ch:string-fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:string-fisher">[ch:string-fisher]</a>, and $b\le Cr\epsilon^{-4}$ yields $$\mathcal F_L(P;h,h')
 \le C\bigl(\epsilon^{-2}+N_1/r\bigr)d(h,h')
 \le Cr\epsilon^{-6}d(h,h').
 \label{ch:later-fisher}$$

It remains to check the regularity promised in the channel definition. For a fixed finite input support $S$, only finitely many centers $z$ can have $D_z\ne0$, all in the $2r$ neighborhood of $S$. For each such center, the alphabet and string set are finite and independent of $P$. The functions $S(P;z,w)$ and $\pi_z(P)_w$ are smooth functions of the coordinates of $P$: the scores are linear, and each exponential sum has a strictly positive denominator. Thus <a href="#ch:later-channel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:later-channel">[ch:later-channel]</a> is a fixed finite smooth formula on each enlarged support space. If $L_{z,w}=0$, strict positivity of $\pi_z(w)$ implies $D_z=0$; also $\dot D_z=0$ by Lemma <a href="#ch:selector-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:selector-estimates">10</a>. Consequently $\dot L_{z,w}=0$. This proves smoothness at faces and the required behavior at zero entries.

<details id="proof-stmt-ch-string-estimates-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-ch-string-estimates</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Proposition <a href="#ch:summary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:summary">8</a>.* Use the same selector $A$ in both versions. Inequality <a href="#ch:selector-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:selector-concentration">[ch:selector-concentration]</a> gives <a href="#ch:selection-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:selection-bound">[ch:selection-bound]</a>. The early construction <a href="#ch:early-channel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:early-channel">[ch:early-channel]</a> gives the two prediction inequalities with errors $0$ and $2\epsilon$; the later construction <a href="#ch:later-channel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:later-channel">[ch:later-channel]</a> gives errors $5\epsilon$ and $6\epsilon$. All are bounded by $10\epsilon$. The Fisher estimates <a href="#ch:selector-fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:selector-fisher">[ch:selector-fisher]</a>, <a href="#ch:early-fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:early-fisher">[ch:early-fisher]</a>, and <a href="#ch:later-fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:later-fisher">[ch:later-fisher]</a> imply <a href="#ch:summary-fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:summary-fisher">[ch:summary-fisher]</a>, since $r\ge1$ and $\epsilon\le1$. The preceding constructions also verify the channel and cross-kernel requirements, including the assertions at zero coordinates. ◻

</details>

For use with differential forms, regard the labels of $A$ and $L$ as disjoint even when their underlying sets happen to coincide. On their disjoint union define the symmetric kernel $K$ by <a id="ch:symmetric-kernel"></a>$$\begin{split}
 K(y,\omega)=K(\omega,y)&=\tfrac12\kappa(y,\omega)
       \quad(y\in G,\ \omega\in\Lambda),\\
 K&=0\quad\text{within either channel}.
 \end{split}
 \label{ch:symmetric-kernel}$$ For vectors on this union, write $K(U,V)=\sum_{\xi,\eta}K(\xi,\eta)U_\xi V_\eta$. The combined field $Y=A\oplus L$ has mass at most two, and <a id="ch:kernel-diagonal"></a>$$K(Y(P),Y(P))
    =\sum_yA(P)_y\operatorname{pred}(P,y).
 \label{ch:kernel-diagonal}$$ The kernel is bounded and invariant under simultaneous left translation. The expression in <a href="#ch:fisher-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:fisher-definition">[ch:fisher-definition]</a> also makes sense for this field of mass at most two, and its square is $\mathcal F_A(P;h,h')^2+\mathcal F_L(P;h,h')^2$. Thus Proposition <a href="#ch:summary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:summary">8</a> controls its derivatives as well. These facts permit both channels to enter a single bilinear pairing in the averaging construction.

<a id="sec:connection"></a>
### A connection from successive time averages

Proposition <a href="#cy:trace-identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:trace-identity">6</a> allows us to compute the trace with any connection satisfying Definition <a href="#cy:connection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:connection">5</a>. We now construct a connection whose curvature can be estimated at several time scales. The construction first gives a one-form $B$ with uniformly positive contraction $B(V)$; division by this contraction will produce the connection. The summary channels of Proposition <a href="#ch:summary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:summary">8</a> make the contributions of successive scales telescope. The final contribution uses the twist by $g$.

Throughout this section $G$ is finitely generated, $d$ is its fixed left invariant word metric, and $g\in G$. Constants denoted by $C$ may depend on the word metric and on the fixed smooth functions used below. After the parameter choice at the end of the construction, a subscript $R$ will permit dependence on the radius $R$, but never on the tuple, its dimension, or the element $g$.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Time averages and spatial summaries

<a id="co:scale-support"></a>
Begin with a finite number $b_*\geq2$ of averaging stages, an even ambient dimension $n$, and positive time scales $\delta_1<\cdots<\delta_{b_*}$ satisfying $$\sum_{i=1}^j\delta_i\leq2\delta_j,
 \qquad 10\delta_{b_*}<1.
 \label{co:scale-support}$$ The construction through positivity uses only these conditions on the time scales. Their precise dependence on $R$ and $n$ will be selected afterwards, when preparing the quantitative estimates. *The same scales are kept fixed in every dimension $0\leq k\leq n$.* Thus passing to a face never changes the averaging operation.

Choose a nonnegative, even $C^\infty$ function $\mu$, supported in $[-1,1]$, with integral one, and put $\mu_j(s)=\delta_j^{-1}\mu(s/\delta_j)$. On a tuple of dimension $k$, write its step function and its averages as <a id="co:averages"></a>$$\begin{split}
 f(u)&=h_i\quad(t_i\leq u<t_{i+1}),\qquad f(u+1)=gf(u),\\
 P_0(u)&=\delta_{f(u)},\qquad
 P_j(u)=\int_{\mathbb R}\mu_j(s)P_{j-1}(u-s)\,ds.
 \end{split}
 \label{co:averages}$$ Zero-length intervals are omitted. These are finitely supported probability distributions at each time, and $P_j(u+1)=gP_j(u)$. If $\eta_j=\mu_j*\cdots*\mu_1$, then <a id="co:smearing"></a>$$P_j=\eta_j*P_0,\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\eta_j\subset[-2\delta_j,2\delta_j],\qquad
 \|\eta_j\|_\infty\leq C/\delta_j.
 \label{co:smearing}$$ The support bound follows from <a href="#co:scale-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:scale-support">[co:scale-support]</a>, and the supremum bound follows by convolving $\mu_j$ with probability densities.

<a id="co:spatial-radii"></a>
<a id="co:error-budget"></a>
To summarize these averages spatially, choose $R\geq1$ and an integer $2\leq J_*\leq b_*$. Put $$r_j=6^{\min(j,J_*)-1}R,\qquad r=6^{J_*-1}R.
 \label{co:spatial-radii}$$ The radius increases by a factor of six during the first $J_*-1$ steps and is then fixed at $r$. Use accuracies $$\epsilon_j=\epsilon_*(1+j)^{-2}\quad(j\geq1),
 \qquad 30\sum_{j\geq1}\epsilon_j\leq\frac1{10},
 \label{co:error-budget}$$ where $0<\epsilon_*\leq10^{-3}$ is fixed sufficiently small. The summable errors will leave a positive quantity after telescoping.

<a id="co:channel-pairing"></a>
For $1\leq j<b_*$, apply Proposition <a href="#ch:summary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:summary">8</a> to $P_j(u)$ at parameters $(r_j,\epsilon_j)$. Use its early version when $j<J_*$ and its later version when $j\geq J_*$. Denote the resulting channels by $A_j,L_j$ and their prediction kernel by $\kappa_j$. On the disjoint union of the two label sets put <a id="co:channel-fields"></a>$$Y_j(u)=A_j(P_j(u))\oplus L_j(P_j(u)).
 \label{co:channel-fields}$$ Define a symmetric bilinear kernel $K_j$ to be zero between labels in the same channel and to have value $\kappa_j(y,\omega)/2$ between the two channels. Thus $$K_j(Y_j(u),Y_j(u))
 =\sum_y A_j(P_j(u))_y\operatorname{pred}_j(P_j(u),y).
 \label{co:channel-pairing}$$ The total mass of $Y_j(u)$ is at most two, and $|K_j|\leq1$.

At the first and last stages use <a id="co:end-fields"></a>$$\begin{array}{lll}
 Y_0=P_0,&
 K_0(h,h')=\chi_R(d(h,h')),\\[2pt]
 Y_{b_*}(u)=D(P_{b_*}(u)),&
 K_{b_*}(h,h')=\mathbf1_{h=h'},
 \end{array}
 \label{co:end-fields}$$ where $D$ is the channel of Lemma <a href="#ch:terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:terminal">11</a> at parameters $(r,\epsilon_{b_*})$. These two fields have mass at most one. All the fields obey $Y_j(u+1)=gY_j(u)$, and every kernel is invariant under simultaneous left translation of its labels.

<a id="co:accuracy"></a>
<a id="co:terminal-multiplier"></a>
<a id="co:twist-assumption"></a>
The last channel needs a multiplier to compensate for its possible spread over many labels. Lemma <a href="#ch:terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:terminal">11</a> gives $\sum_zD(P)_z^2\geq\frac14e^{-C_Dr}$ when $\phi_r(P)>0$, for a fixed constant $C_D$. Choose a fixed $C_*\geq C_D$ and set $$W=\exp(C_*r).
 \label{co:accuracy}$$ Since $0\leq\phi_r\leq1-a_*=1/5$, this gives, for every accuracy parameter in <a href="#co:error-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:error-budget">[co:error-budget]</a>, $$W\sum_zD(P)_z^2\geq\phi_r(P).
 \label{co:terminal-multiplier}$$ We shall construct the connection under either of the assumptions $$\begin{array}{ll}
 \textnormal{(i)}&g\text{ has infinite order},\\[2pt]
 \textnormal{(ii)}&g\text{ has finite order and }
                \displaystyle\min_{h\in G}d(h,gh)>4r.
 \end{array}
 \label{co:twist-assumption}$$ Only the final time-lag kernel and its contraction distinguish these two cases.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Lag pairings and their compatibility

<a id="co:lag-derivative"></a>
We next turn the fields into one-forms. For $0\leq j<b_*$ let $\rho_j=\mu_{j+1}*\mu_{j+1}$ and define the odd function <a id="co:lag-kernels"></a>$$U_j(s)=\operatorname{sign}(s)-2\int_0^s\rho_j(a)\,da.
 \label{co:lag-kernels}$$ It is bounded in absolute value by one and vanishes outside $[-2\delta_{j+1},2\delta_{j+1}]$. In the sense of distributions, $$U_j'=2(\delta_0-\rho_j).
 \label{co:lag-derivative}$$ Here $\delta_0$ denotes the Dirac measure at zero, rather than a time scale. At the final stage set <a id="co:last-kernel"></a>$$\begin{array}{lll}
 U_{b_*}(s)=\operatorname{sign}(s),&\rho_{b_*}=0,
 &\text{in case (i)},\\[2pt]
 U_{b_*}(s)=\operatorname{sign}(s)\mathbf1_{|s|<1},&
 \rho_{b_*}=\tfrac12(\delta_{-1}+\delta_1),
 &\text{in case (ii)}.
 \end{array}
 \label{co:last-kernel}$$ Formula <a href="#co:lag-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:lag-derivative">[co:lag-derivative]</a> holds at this stage as well. Values at the jump points have no effect on any integral below.

<a id="co:forms"></a>
<a id="co:impulses"></a>
<a id="co:raw-coefficient"></a>
The differential $dY_j(u)$ in the following definition acts on the tuple times $t_0,\ldots,t_k$, with $u$ fixed. Write $\int_{\rm per}$ for integration over one period, using a half-open interval when integrating the raw impulse measure, and put $$\begin{split}
 B_j&=-\frac12\int_{\rm per}du\int_{\mathbb R}dv\,
             U_j(v-u)K_j(dY_j(u),Y_j(v)),\\
 B&=\sum_{j=0}^{b_*-1}B_j+WB_{b_*}.
 \end{split}
 \label{co:forms}$$ For $j=0$ the differential of the step field is a measure in $u$. Specifically, set $$J_i=\delta_{h_{i-1}}-\delta_{h_i},\qquad
 dP_0(u)=\sum_{i=0}^k\sum_{a\in\mathbb Z}
                  g^aJ_i\,\delta_{t_i+a}(u)\,dt_i.
 \label{co:impulses}$$ The periodic convention gives $h_{-1}=g^{-1}h_k$. Thus the coefficient of $dt_i$ in the raw form is $$(B_0)_i=-\frac12\int_{\mathbb R}U_0(v-t_i)K_0(J_i,P_0(v))\,dv.
 \label{co:raw-coefficient}$$ The expression in <a href="#co:impulses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:impulses">[co:impulses]</a> is a distributional identity in the interior of the ordered time domain. Formula <a href="#co:raw-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:raw-coefficient">[co:raw-coefficient]</a> will also specify its smooth extension to the faces.

<a id="stmt-co-regularity"></a>
<div id="co:regularity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14**. *The forms in <a href="#co:forms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:forms">[co:forms]</a> are well defined, independent of the period cut, and smooth up to every simplex face by one-sided extension. They are invariant under simultaneous translation of all times, under the centralizer action, and under twisted rotation. Their tangential restrictions to faces equal the forms for the shortened tuples, using the same parameters and the same fixed integer $n$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-regularity"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-regularity</summary>

<a id="co:smooth-intervals"></a>
*Proof.* We first treat $j>0$. On a fixed tuple the smoothed probabilities have the explicit expression $$P_j(u)=\sum_{a\in\mathbb Z}\sum_{i=0}^k
 \left(\int_{t_i+a}^{t_{i+1}+a}\eta_j(u-v)\,dv\right)\delta_{g^ah_i}.
 \label{co:smooth-intervals}$$ For $u$ in a bounded interval and times in a neighborhood of any point of the closed ordered domain, only a fixed finite set of summands occurs. Each coefficient is smooth in $u$ and the times, including when the endpoints coincide. The channel maps are smooth on the finite probability simplices, with smooth extensions at their faces, so $Y_j$ has the same regularity. Its possible labels on one period lie in a fixed finite set locally in the time variables.

When $U_j$ has compact support, the two integrals in <a href="#co:forms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:forms">[co:forms]</a> can therefore be differentiated under fixed integration domains. The final full-sign kernel in case (i) requires an additional observation. Write $v=w+a$ with $w$ in one period. If $y,z$ are possible labels in that period, then $$K_{b_*}(y,g^az)\ne0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad y=g^az.$$ Since $g$ has infinite order, this equation has at most one solution $a\in\mathbb Z$ for each pair $(y,z)$. There are only finitely many such pairs locally. The full-sign integral is therefore locally a finite sum of smooth integrals over bounded domains, even though the possible matching integers need not be uniformly bounded as the tuple varies.

<a id="co:cut-cancellation"></a>
To check the period cut, regard $$F_j(u)=\int_{\mathbb R}U_j(v-u)K_j(dY_j(u),Y_j(v))\,dv$$ as a one-form in the time parameters. The identities $Y_j(u+1)=gY_j(u)$ and $dY_j(u+1)=g\,dY_j(u)$, followed by the change of variable $v\mapsto v+1$, give $F_j(u+1)=F_j(u)$. Hence its integral over one period is independent of the cut. This also shows why a moving cut does not introduce an extra exterior-derivative term: if the cut is $c=c(t)$, the endpoint contribution is $$dc\wedge\bigl(F_j(c+1)-F_j(c)\bigr)=0.
 \label{co:cut-cancellation}$$

<a id="co:raw-primitive"></a>
For the raw stage, work first away from the faces and choose a fixed period cut away from its finitely many jumps. Evaluating the impulse measure gives <a href="#co:raw-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:raw-coefficient">[co:raw-coefficient]</a>; moving an impulse to another period leaves this coefficient unchanged, by equivariance and a change of variable. To prove one-sided smoothness explicitly, let $$Q(s)=\int_0^sU_0(w)\,dw
 =|s|-2\int_0^s\int_0^w\rho_0(a)\,da\,dw.$$ Then $(B_0)_i$ is a locally finite sum of terms $$-\frac12K_0(J_i,\delta_{g^ah_q})
 \bigl(Q(t_{q+1}+a-t_i)-Q(t_q+a-t_i)\bigr),
 \quad 0\leq q\leq k.
 \label{co:raw-primitive}$$ Although $Q$ is constant rather than zero outside the lag support, each displayed endpoint difference vanishes when its interval misses that support. Since all boundaries lie in one period and $2\delta_1<1$, only the shifts $a=-1,0,1$ can contribute, with the same finite choice valid in a sufficiently small neighborhood of any point of the closed domain. On the entire closed ordered domain, each affine expression $t_q+a-t_i$ has a fixed weak sign: the order of the boundaries and all their period translates is prescribed. The same is true for $t_{q+1}+a-t_i$. Thus every absolute value in <a href="#co:raw-primitive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:raw-primitive">[co:raw-primitive]</a> equals a fixed signed affine function on that domain. The remaining part of $Q$ is smooth on the whole line. Consequently the coefficient has a smooth extension from the prescribed side at each face, including intersections of faces. Compact support of $U_0$ allows the finite set of summands to be fixed in a neighborhood.

It remains to identify the restrictions. On the face $t_i=t_{i+1}$, where $0\leq i<k$, the interval labelled $h_i$ disappears and the adjoining impulses combine tangentially as $$J_i\,dt_i+J_{i+1}\,dt_{i+1}
 =\bigl(\delta_{h_{i-1}}-\delta_{h_{i+1}}\bigr)dt_i.$$ This is precisely the impulse for the shortened tuple. On the closing face $t_k=t_0+1$, combine the impulses at that common lift: $$J_k+gJ_0
 =\delta_{h_{k-1}}-\delta_{gh_0}.$$ Their time differentials agree tangentially, and the displayed sum is the closing impulse after deleting $h_k$. These identities also handle corners by successive merging. In the smoothed stages, the underlying step function itself is unchanged by deleting a zero-length interval; <a href="#co:smooth-intervals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:smooth-intervals">[co:smooth-intervals]</a> then gives equality of the fields and their tangential differentials. Hence every $B_j$ respects the faces.

Finally, translating all times by $s$ replaces $Y_j(u)$ by $Y_j(u-s)$. Changing both integration variables by $s$ and using cut independence proves translation invariance. The centralizer action preserves the twist and all pairings. Twisted rotation describes the very same step function with a different first boundary, so it too preserves the forms. These arguments also establish cut independence of the raw form on the faces, by continuity. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Contraction and the positive normalizer

The forms are now compatible on all tuple simplices. To normalize them to a connection, we must show that their sum has positive contraction with the translation vector $V$. The choice of the lag derivative in <a href="#co:lag-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:lag-derivative">[co:lag-derivative]</a> converts this contraction into a difference between a pairing at one time and its average at the next scale.

<a id="stmt-co-contraction-lemma"></a>
<div id="co:contraction-lemma" class="lemma">

<a id="co:contraction"></a>
<a id="co:averaged-pairing"></a>
**Lemma 15**. *For every stage $0\leq j\leq b_*$, $$C_j:=B_j(V)
 =\int_{\rm per}\left(
 K_j(Y_j(u),Y_j(u))
 -\int_{\mathbb R}K_j(Y_j(u),Y_j(u+s))\,d\rho_j(s)\right)du.
 \label{co:contraction}$$ For $j<b_*$, the second integrated pairing is also $$\int_{\rm per}
 K_j\bigl((\mu_{j+1}*Y_j)(u),(\mu_{j+1}*Y_j)(u)\bigr)\,du.
 \label{co:averaged-pairing}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-contraction-lemma"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-contraction-lemma</summary>

*Proof.* Translation of the times translates the field in the opposite direction, so $dY_j(V)=-\partial_uY_j$. Define the scalar function $$H_j(u)=\int_{\mathbb R}U_j(v-u)K_j(Y_j(u),Y_j(v))\,dv.$$ As in the proof of Lemma <a href="#co:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:regularity">14</a>, it is periodic. For a smooth stage its distributional derivative is $$H_j'(u)
 =-\int U_j'(v-u)K_j(Y_j(u),Y_j(v))\,dv
  +\int U_j(v-u)K_j(\partial_uY_j(u),Y_j(v))\,dv.$$ Its derivative integrates to zero over a period. Substitution into <a href="#co:forms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:forms">[co:forms]</a>, followed by <a href="#co:lag-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:lag-derivative">[co:lag-derivative]</a>, gives <a href="#co:contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:contraction">[co:contraction]</a>, with the factor $1/2$ cancelling the factor two in $U_j'$.

The same computation is valid for $Y_0$ with its jumps. Indeed, for each label $y$, the function $$u\longmapsto\int U_0(v-u)K_0(\delta_y,P_0(v))\,dv$$ is continuous and has bounded weak derivative obtained by replacing $U_0$ by $-U_0'$. Integration by parts against the jump measure is therefore valid. A cut away from the jumps makes the boundary cancellation ordinary periodicity; the result extends to the faces by Lemma <a href="#co:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:regularity">14</a>.

For <a href="#co:averaged-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:averaged-pairing">[co:averaged-pairing]</a>, expand both convolutions. If their lags are $a,b$, simultaneous translation in the period integral changes $K_j(Y_j(u-a),Y_j(u-b))$ into $K_j(Y_j(u),Y_j(u+a-b))$. This is legitimate because the scalar pairing is invariant under simultaneous integer shifts of its two time arguments. The distribution of $a-b$ is $\mu_{j+1}*\mu_{j+1}=\rho_j$, since $\mu_{j+1}$ is even. This gives the asserted identity. ◻

</details>

Write $$\Phi_j=\int_{\rm per}\phi_{r_j}(P_j(u))\,du
 \qquad(1\leq j\leq b_*).$$ These are the concentration quantities that will telescope.

<a id="stmt-co-positivity"></a>
<div id="co:positivity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16**. *Under <a href="#co:twist-assumption" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:twist-assumption">[co:twist-assumption]</a>, the scalar function <a id="co:normalizer"></a>$$C_{\rm nor}:=B(V)=\sum_{j=0}^{b_*-1}C_j+WC_{b_*}
 \label{co:normalizer}$$ satisfies $C_{\rm nor}\geq c_0$, where $c_0=1/10$ is independent of all tuples, $n$, $R$, and $g$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-positivity"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-positivity</summary>

*Proof.* Consider first $1\leq j<b_*$. The concentration-loss calculation <a href="#ch:averaging-drop" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ch:averaging-drop">[ch:averaging-drop]</a> takes the following form for our time averages. In this paragraph abbreviate $A_j(P_j(u))$ to $A(u)$ and the prediction $\operatorname{pred}_j(P_j(u),y)$ to $p(u,y)$. Put $\overline A=\mu_{j+1}*A$ and $\overline p(\cdot,y)=\mu_{j+1}*p(\cdot,y)$. Equations <a href="#co:channel-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:channel-pairing">[co:channel-pairing]</a> and <a href="#co:averaged-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:averaged-pairing">[co:averaged-pairing]</a> give $$C_j=\int_{\rm per}\sum_y A(u)_y p(u,y)\,du
      -\int_{\rm per}\sum_y\overline A(u)_y\overline p(u,y)\,du.$$ The total mass of $A(u)$ is a periodic scalar function. Averaging preserves its integral, so we may subtract $a_*$ from both predictions: <a id="co:subtract-threshold"></a>$$C_j=\int_{\rm per}\sum_y A(u)_y(p(u,y)-a_*)\,du
      -\int_{\rm per}\sum_y\overline A(u)_y
                         (\overline p(u,y)-a_*)\,du.
 \label{co:subtract-threshold}$$ This cancellation uses the integrated mass; it does not require either channel to have mass one at each time.

<a id="co:telescope"></a>
The first and third inequalities of Proposition <a href="#ch:summary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:summary">8</a> imply $$\sum_y A(u)_y(p(u,y)-a_*)
 \geq\phi_{r_j}(P_j(u))-20\epsilon_j.$$ For the other term, the prediction upper bound, linearity of $E_{r_{j+1}}$ in its probability argument, and $P_{j+1}=\mu_{j+1}*P_j$ imply $$\overline p(u,y)-a_*
 \leq E_{r_{j+1}}(P_{j+1}(u),y)-a_*+10\epsilon_j.$$ Here the radius is $r_{j+1}=6r_j$ for the early version and $r_{j+1}=r_j$ for the later version, exactly as required by the two channel bounds. Since $\overline A(u)$ is a subprobability distribution, pairing this inequality with it yields an upper bound $\phi_{r_{j+1}}(P_{j+1}(u))+10\epsilon_j$. Consequently $$C_j\geq\Phi_j-\Phi_{j+1}-30\epsilon_j
 \qquad(1\leq j<b_*).
 \label{co:telescope}$$

<a id="co:initial-contraction"></a>
The raw stage supplies the initial positive quantity. Its same-time pairing is one, while <a href="#co:averaged-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:averaged-pairing">[co:averaged-pairing]</a> gives $K_0(P_1(u),P_1(u))$ for the averaged pairing. For every probability distribution $P$, $$K_0(P,P)=\sum_yP_yE_R(P,y)\leq q_R(P)
 \leq a_*+\phi_R(P).$$ Therefore $$C_0\geq1-a_*-\Phi_1=\frac15-\Phi_1.
 \label{co:initial-contraction}$$

At the final stage the negative term in <a href="#co:contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:contraction">[co:contraction]</a> vanishes. This is immediate from $\rho_{b_*}=0$ in case (i). In case (ii), let $S=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}D(P_{b_*}(u))$. Lemma <a href="#ch:terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:terminal">11</a> gives $\operatorname{diam}S\leq4r$. If $z=gw$ with $z,w\in S$, then $d(w,gw)\leq4r$, contrary to <a href="#co:twist-assumption" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:twist-assumption">[co:twist-assumption]</a>. Thus $S\cap gS=S\cap g^{-1}S=\varnothing$, and twist equivariance makes both lag pairings at $s=\pm1$ zero. In either case, $$C_{b_*}=\int_{\rm per}\sum_zD(P_{b_*}(u))_z^2\,du,
 \qquad WC_{b_*}\geq\Phi_{b_*}$$ by <a href="#co:terminal-multiplier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:terminal-multiplier">[co:terminal-multiplier]</a>. Adding this inequality to <a href="#co:telescope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:telescope">[co:telescope]</a> and <a href="#co:initial-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:initial-contraction">[co:initial-contraction]</a> leaves $$C_{\rm nor}\geq\frac15-30\sum_{j=1}^{b_*-1}\epsilon_j
 \geq\frac1{10},$$ as claimed. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Differential bounds and the resulting connection

Positivity now allows the normalization. Before applying the trace identity, we also need growth bounds that justify summation over all tuples. More precise bounds on the individual channel differentials will provide the input for the curvature estimates of the next section.

We now specialize the parameters of the preceding construction. At the shortest time scales we use the early channels, whose derivative bound is independent of the spatial radius. The later channels keep that radius fixed; this also fixes the terminal multiplier $W$ while the time scales continue to increase. Their longer time scales will absorb the polynomial factor $r^{10}$ in the later derivative bound.

<a id="co:ladder"></a>
<a id="co:last-scale"></a>
<a id="co:time-scales"></a>
Choose once and for all an integer $J_*$ with $(3/2)^{J_*-1}>200$. For sufficiently large $R$ retain the spatial radii, accuracies, and multiplier from <a href="#co:spatial-radii" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:spatial-radii">[co:spatial-radii]</a>, <a href="#co:error-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:error-budget">[co:error-budget]</a>, and <a href="#co:accuracy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:accuracy">[co:accuracy]</a>, and set $$\beta_1=R,\qquad \beta_{j+1}=\beta_j^{3/2}.
 \label{co:ladder}$$ Choose $b_*$ to be the smallest integer such that $$b_*\geq J_*,\qquad \beta_{b_*}\geq W^2R^{100}.
 \label{co:last-scale}$$ For each even integer $n>10\beta_{b_*}$, use $$\delta_j=\beta_j/n\qquad(1\leq j\leq b_*).
 \label{co:time-scales}$$ The ratio $\beta_{j+1}/\beta_j$ is at least two when $R$ is sufficiently large, so these scales satisfy <a href="#co:scale-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:scale-support">[co:scale-support]</a>. Thus the construction and positivity proved above apply. For each such $n$, the same actual scales $\beta_j/n$ are used in every dimension $k\leq n$; face restriction never changes the denominator to $k$.

<a id="co:stage-count"></a>
The stage count, spatial radii, accuracies, multiplier, and numbers $\beta_j$ now depend only on $R$ and the fixed choices. Since $\log\beta_j=(3/2)^{j-1}\log R$ and $\log(W^2R^{100})=2C_*r+100\log R$, with $r$ a fixed multiple of $R$, $$b_*=O(1+\log R).
 \label{co:stage-count}$$ The particular exponents in <a href="#co:ladder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:ladder">[co:ladder]</a> and <a href="#co:last-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:last-scale">[co:last-scale]</a> will enter the curvature bounds. They leave room for the polynomial losses in the channel derivatives.

<a id="co:derivative-constants"></a>
Choose a sufficiently large fixed constant $C$ and set $$a_j=C\epsilon_j^{-10}
 \begin{cases}
 1,&1\leq j<J_*,\\
 r^{10},&J_*\leq j\leq b_*.
 \end{cases}
 \label{co:derivative-constants}$$ As before, $l_i=1+d(h_{i-1},h_i)$, with periodic indexing, and $\partial_i=\partial/\partial t_i$.

<a id="stmt-co-connection"></a>
<div id="co:connection" class="proposition">

<a id="co:fisher"></a>
<a id="co:coefficients"></a>
<a id="co:normalized"></a>
**Proposition 17**. *Let $R$ be sufficiently large, choose the parameters in <a href="#co:ladder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:ladder">[co:ladder]</a> and <a href="#co:last-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:last-scale">[co:last-scale]</a>, and let $n>10\beta_{b_*}$ be even, with time scales <a href="#co:time-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:time-scales">[co:time-scales]</a>. Suppose that $g$ satisfies <a href="#co:twist-assumption" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:twist-assumption">[co:twist-assumption]</a>. The forms $B_j$ and $B$ in <a href="#co:forms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:forms">[co:forms]</a>, constructed in all dimensions $0\leq k\leq n$ with the same parameters, have the following properties. For every smooth stage $1\leq j\leq b_*$ and every coordinate $t_i$, $$\left(\int_{\rm per}\sum_\omega
        \frac{|\partial_iY_j(u)_\omega|^2}{Y_j(u)_\omega}\,du
 \right)^{1/2}
 \leq\frac{a_jl_i}{\sqrt{\delta_j}}.
 \label{co:fisher}$$ Terms with zero denominator are defined to be zero. Moreover, $$|B(\partial_i)|+|\partial_iC_{\rm nor}|\leq C_Rl_i.
 \label{co:coefficients}$$ The scalar $C_{\rm nor}$ is at least $c_0=1/10$, and $$\alpha=\frac{B}{C_{\rm nor}}
 \label{co:normalized}$$ is a compatible connection family in the sense of Definition <a href="#cy:connection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:connection">5</a>. Its coefficients and those of its exterior derivative are bounded by polynomials in the lengths, uniformly over the tuples. In particular, the family may be used in Proposition <a href="#cy:trace-identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:trace-identity">6</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-connection"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-connection</summary>

<a id="co:smeared-impulse"></a>
*Proof.* Differentiating <a href="#co:smooth-intervals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:smooth-intervals">[co:smooth-intervals]</a> at a boundary gives the smeared impulse formula $$\partial_iP_j(u)
 =\sum_{a\in\mathbb Z}\eta_j(u-t_i-a)g^aJ_i.
 \label{co:smeared-impulse}$$ For each $u$, at most one summand can be nonzero, because $4\delta_j<1$. The derivative is supported within $2\delta_j$ of $t_i$ modulo periods. The distance between the two labels in $g^aJ_i$ is $d(h_{i-1},h_i)$, independently of $a$. The derivative estimate of Proposition <a href="#ch:summary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:summary">8</a>, or Lemma <a href="#ch:terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:terminal">11</a> at the last stage, therefore bounds the square root of the pointwise sum in <a href="#co:fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:fisher">[co:fisher]</a> by $C\epsilon_j^{-10}l_i\eta_j(u-t_i-a)$ for $j<J_*$, with an additional factor $r^{10}$ for $j\geq J_*$, where $a$ is the contributing integer. Since $$\int_{\rm per}\sum_a\eta_j(u-t_i-a)^2\,du
 =\int_{\mathbb R}\eta_j(s)^2\,ds
 \leq\|\eta_j\|_\infty\leq C/\delta_j,$$ we obtain <a href="#co:fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:fisher">[co:fisher]</a> after fixing the constant in <a href="#co:derivative-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:derivative-constants">[co:derivative-constants]</a>. The two disjoint channels only alter this constant. At a zero channel weight the first derivative is zero, as stated in Proposition <a href="#ch:summary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ch:summary">8</a>, so the convention in <a href="#co:fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:fisher">[co:fisher]</a> is consistent.

<a id="co:integrated-derivative"></a>
The support information is useful as well as the square-integral bound. The support of $\partial_iY_j$ has measure at most $4\delta_j$ in a period, and the field has mass at most two. Cauchy–Schwarz in time and labels consequently gives $$\int_{\rm per}\sum_\omega|\partial_iY_j(u)_\omega|\,du
 \leq C a_jl_i.
 \label{co:integrated-derivative}$$ For example, the square of the factor paired with <a href="#co:fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:fisher">[co:fisher]</a> is at most $\int_{\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\partial_iY_j}\sum_\omega Y_j(u)_\omega\,du
\leq8\delta_j$. This explains why the integrated derivative loses no power of the time scale.

<a id="co:orbit-mass"></a>
We now bound the coefficients of $B$. For a compact lag kernel, the inner integral in <a href="#co:forms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:forms">[co:forms]</a> has length at most two, its kernel is bounded, and the field has bounded mass at every time. Thus <a href="#co:integrated-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:integrated-derivative">[co:integrated-derivative]</a> gives $|B_j(\partial_i)|\leq Ca_jl_i$. At the full-sign stage there is no bound on the length of the integration interval, but equality of labels gives a stronger estimate. For each fixed group label $y$, $$\int_{\mathbb R}Y_{b_*}(v)_y\,dv
 =\int_0^1\sum_{a\in\mathbb Z}Y_{b_*}(v)_{g^{-a}y}\,dv
 \leq1.
 \label{co:orbit-mass}$$ The labels $g^{-a}y$ are distinct because $g$ has infinite order, and the total mass at every time is at most one. Combining <a href="#co:orbit-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:orbit-mass">[co:orbit-mass]</a> with <a href="#co:integrated-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:integrated-derivative">[co:integrated-derivative]</a> proves the same coefficient bound. In particular, the estimate does not depend on how far apart the finitely many matching periods are.

Differentiating <a href="#co:contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:contraction">[co:contraction]</a> gives $|\partial_iC_j|\leq Ca_jl_i$ for $j>0$. Indeed, each differentiation puts a derivative on one of the two factors. The other factor has bounded total mass, the measure $\rho_j$ has mass at most one, and <a href="#co:integrated-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:integrated-derivative">[co:integrated-derivative]</a> is unchanged by a time shift. The last observation follows because the sum of absolute derivatives is a periodic scalar function.

For the raw stage, <a href="#co:raw-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:raw-coefficient">[co:raw-coefficient]</a> bounds $|(B_0)_i|\leq C$, since $\|J_i\|_1\leq2$ and the lag support has length at most $4\delta_1<1$. Its contraction satisfies the exact identity $$C_0=1-\int_{\rm per}K_0(P_1(u),P_1(u))\,du.$$ Formula <a href="#co:smeared-impulse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:smeared-impulse">[co:smeared-impulse]</a> for $P_1$, before passing through any channel map, gives $\int_{\rm per}\|\partial_iP_1(u)\|_1\,du\leq2$. Hence $|\partial_iC_0|\leq4$. Summing these estimates with the coefficients $1,\ldots,1,W$ proves <a href="#co:coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:coefficients">[co:coefficients]</a>, because $$1+\sum_{j=1}^{b_*-1}a_j+Wa_{b_*}$$ depends only on $R$ and the fixed choices.

<a id="co:exterior-pairing"></a>
<a id="co:coarse-curvature"></a>
For completeness, polynomial control of exterior derivatives can be obtained here without using the finer estimates of the next section. For a smooth stage, differentiation at a fixed period cut gives $$dB_j=\frac12\int_{\rm per}du\int_{\mathbb R}dv\,
          U_j(v-u)K_j(dY_j(u)\wedge dY_j(v)).
 \label{co:exterior-pairing}$$ There is no term from the cut by <a href="#co:cut-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:cut-cancellation">[co:cut-cancellation]</a>. For a compact lag kernel, reduction of both time variables to one period introduces at most three relevant period shifts. Boundedness of the kernel and <a href="#co:integrated-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:integrated-derivative">[co:integrated-derivative]</a> therefore imply $$|dB_j(\partial_i,\partial_{i'})|
 \leq Ca_j^2l_il_{i'}.$$ For the full-sign equality kernel, at most one shift contributes for each pair of labels, and the same estimate follows. In the raw stage, differentiate <a href="#co:raw-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:raw-coefficient">[co:raw-coefficient]</a> with respect to a distinct boundary. The resulting terms have factors $U_0(t_{i'}+a-t_i)K_0(J_i,g^aJ_{i'})$; boundedness of $U_0,K_0$ and the jump masses gives a uniform bound. Differentiation of a coefficient in its own time variable contributes no exterior two-form. These calculations hold in the interior and extend to the closed simplex by Lemma <a href="#co:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:regularity">14</a>. Summation over stages gives $$|dB(\partial_i,\partial_{i'})|\leq C_Rl_il_{i'}.
 \label{co:coarse-curvature}$$

Finally Lemma <a href="#co:positivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:positivity">16</a> permits division by $C_{\rm nor}$. The scalar normalizer inherits all symmetries and face restrictions of $B$, so <a href="#co:normalized" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:normalized">[co:normalized]</a> does also, and $\alpha(V)=1$. Equations <a href="#co:coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:coefficients">[co:coefficients]</a>, <a href="#co:coarse-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:coarse-curvature">[co:coarse-curvature]</a>, and $$d\alpha=C_{\rm nor}^{-1}dB
          -C_{\rm nor}^{-2}dC_{\rm nor}\wedge B$$ give the required polynomial coefficient bounds. The resulting family is therefore a compatible connection family. Its curvature is basic, as also follows directly from translation invariance and $\alpha(V)=1$. ◻

</details>

The construction separates two estimates that the trace argument will use differently. The bounds in Proposition <a href="#co:connection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:connection">17</a> justify the weighted simplex identities, while the scale-dependent bound <a href="#co:fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:fisher">[co:fisher]</a> retains the information needed to make high-dimensional curvature Pfaffians small. We turn to those sharper estimates next.

<a id="sec:curvature"></a>
### Curvature estimates and the Pfaffian bound

We estimate the curvature of the connection constructed in Proposition <a href="#co:connection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:connection">17</a>. The smooth stages are controlled by operators on weighted Hilbert spaces. The raw stage is instead controlled by the number of nearby simplex coordinates. These two estimates will be combined only after averaging over the coordinate subsets in the Pfaffian expansion.

Throughout this section, the word metric and the fixed construction constants are understood. The constant $C$ may depend on these data but not on $R,n,g$, or the tuple. A constant $C_R$ may additionally depend on $R$. All parameters $r,W,b_*,\beta_j,\delta_j$, and $a_j$ retain their definitions from Section <a href="#sec:connection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:connection">5</a>. In particular, $\delta_j=\beta_j/n$ and $10\delta_{b_*}<1$.

<a id="pf:normalization"></a>
We work in the section $t_0=0$, on the simplex $$\Delta_n=\{0\le t_1\le\cdots\le t_n\le1\},
 \qquad n=2m.$$ Expectation with respect to uniform simplex probability will be denoted by $\mathbb E_{\Delta_n}$; its density in these coordinates is $n!$. If $Q$ is a two-form, its matrix is defined by $Q=\sum_{i<i'}Q_{ii'}\,dt_i\wedge dt_{i'}$. Our convention is $$\frac{Q^m}{m!}
 =\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}(Q)\,dt_1\wedge\cdots\wedge dt_n.
 \label{pf:normalization}$$ Equation <a href="#cy:factorials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:factorials">[cy:factorials]</a> identifies the expectation below with the normalized integral in Proposition <a href="#cy:trace-identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:trace-identity">6</a>. For a subset $I\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}$ of even cardinality, $\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}_I(Q)$ means the Pfaffian of the principal matrix on $I$, listed in increasing order. We set $\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}_{\varnothing}(Q)=1$.

<a id="stmt-pf-bound"></a>
<div id="pf:bound" class="theorem">

<a id="pf:main-estimate"></a>
**Theorem 18** (Averaged curvature bound). *Fix a sufficiently large $R$, and set $S=b_*+2$. Let $n$ be an even integer with $n>10\beta_{b_*}$. Suppose either that $g$ has infinite order, or that $g$ has finite order and $$\min_{h\in G}d(h,gh)>4r.$$ For every tuple $(h_0,\ldots,h_n)$, the connection $\alpha=B/C_{\rm nor}$ of Proposition <a href="#co:connection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:connection">17</a> satisfies $$\mathbb E_{\Delta_n}|\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}(d\alpha)|
 \le C_R\bigl(CS^{3/2}R^{-1/10}\bigr)^n
          \prod_{i=1}^n l_i,
 \qquad l_i=1+d(h_{i-1},h_i).
 \label{pf:main-estimate}$$ The constants are independent of $n,g$, and the tuple. Moreover, $S=O(1+\log R)$ as $R\to\infty$.*

</div>

We prove the theorem in three steps: estimates for principal Pfaffians of the smooth stages, an averaged estimate for the raw stage, and the expansion of the normalized curvature.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Smooth stages as weighted operators

<a id="pf:kernel"></a>
Fix a stage $j>0$ and a point of the simplex. Let $\Lambda_j$ be its label set and equip $(0,1)\times\Lambda_j$ with the measure $$d\vartheta_j(u,y)=Y_j(u)_y\,du.$$ We use the real Hilbert space $\mathcal H_j=L^2(\vartheta_j)$. Recall that the total mass of $Y_j(u)$ is at most two, and is at most one at the last stage. Define the operator $T_j$ by the kernel $$T_j(u,y;v,z)
   =\sum_{a\in\mathbb Z}U_j(v+a-u)K_j(y,g^a z),
 \label{pf:kernel}$$ where the action of $g$ on labels is the one used in the channel construction. Oddness of $U_j$, symmetry of $K_j$, and simultaneous equivariance of $K_j$ show that this kernel is skew. Indeed, exchange $(u,y)$ with $(v,z)$ and replace $a$ by $-a$ in the sum.

<a id="pf:vector-norm"></a>
<a id="pf:curvature-pairing"></a>
For $1\le i\le n$, put $$X_i(u,y)=\frac{\partial_iY_j(u)_y}{Y_j(u)_y}
 \quad\text{when }Y_j(u)_y>0,$$ and give $X_i$ the value zero at the remaining points. The derivatives of the zero weights vanish, and <a href="#co:fisher" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:fisher">[co:fisher]</a> gives $$\|X_i\|_{\mathcal H_j}
 \le \frac{a_jl_i}{\sqrt{\delta_j}}.
 \label{pf:vector-norm}$$ The exterior-derivative identity <a href="#co:exterior-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:exterior-pairing">[co:exterior-pairing]</a> is $$dB_j=\frac12\int_{\rm per}du\int_{\mathbb R}dv\,
       U_j(v-u)K_j\bigl(dY_j(u)\wedge dY_j(v)\bigr).$$ The integral over a period can be taken with fixed endpoints because its integrand is periodic. Reducing $v$ to $(0,1)$ gives <a href="#pf:kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:kernel">[pf:kernel]</a>; the two antisymmetric terms then agree. Consequently, $$(dB_j)_{ii'}=\langle X_i,T_jX_{i'}\rangle_{\mathcal H_j}.
 \label{pf:curvature-pairing}$$

<a id="pf:compression"></a>
Here is the finite-dimensional estimate we shall apply to this pairing. Let $I$ have even size $k>0$, let $E$ be the span of the vectors $X_i$, $i\in I$, and let $s_1\ge s_2\ge\cdots$ be the singular values of the compression $P_ET_j|_E$, completed by zeros. Then $$|\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}_I(dB_j)|
 \le \prod_{i\in I}\|X_i\|
       \left(\prod_{\nu=1}^k s_\nu\right)^{1/2}.
 \label{pf:compression}$$ To see this, the matrix in <a href="#pf:curvature-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:curvature-pairing">[pf:curvature-pairing]</a> has rank less than $k$ if $\dim E<k$, so its Pfaffian is zero. Otherwise write the $X_i$ as columns of a square matrix $V$ in an orthonormal basis of $E$. The curvature matrix is $V^{\mathsf T}T_EV$. Taking determinants and using $\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}(A)^2=\det A$ for a skew matrix gives the right side of <a href="#pf:compression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:compression">[pf:compression]</a>, after Hadamard’s inequality $|\det V|\le\prod_i\|X_i\|$.

<a id="pf:intermediate"></a>
For $1\le j<b_*$, the support of $U_j$ is contained in $[-2\delta_{j+1},2\delta_{j+1}]$. At most one integer $a$ in <a href="#pf:kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:kernel">[pf:kernel]</a> contributes for a fixed pair $(u,v)$, because $4\delta_{j+1}<1$. Thus the kernel is bounded by a fixed constant and vanishes unless the circular distance between $u$ and $v$ is at most $2\delta_{j+1}$. The mass bound on the channels implies $$\|T_j\|_{\rm HS}^2
 =\int\!\int |T_j(u,y;v,z)|^2\,
       d\vartheta_j(u,y)d\vartheta_j(v,z)
 \le C\delta_{j+1}.$$ Bessel’s inequality gives the same upper bound for the squared Hilbert–Schmidt norm of every compression. Applying the arithmetic mean inequality to $s_1^2,\ldots,s_k^2$ gives $$\left(\prod_{\nu=1}^k s_\nu\right)^{1/2}
 \le C^k\delta_{j+1}^{k/4}k^{-k/4}.$$ Combining this with <a href="#pf:vector-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:vector-norm">[pf:vector-norm]</a> and <a href="#pf:compression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:compression">[pf:compression]</a>, and using $\beta_{j+1}=\beta_j^{3/2}$, proves $$|\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}_I(dB_j)|
 \le\bigl(Ca_j\beta_j^{-1/8}\bigr)^k
          (n/k)^{k/4}\prod_{i\in I}l_i,
 \qquad 1\le j<b_*.
 \label{pf:intermediate}$$ In fact, the powers of the time scales simplify as $$\delta_j^{-k/2}\delta_{j+1}^{k/4}k^{-k/4}
 =\beta_j^{-k/8}(n/k)^{k/4}.$$

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### The terminal sign kernel

The last lag kernel is not supported at a small time scale. Its useful property is instead that an order comparison is constant between two disjoint ordered intervals. The following elementary approximation works even when the source and target measures differ.

<a id="stmt-pf-order-kernel"></a>
<div id="pf:order-kernel" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (Approximation of order kernels). *Let $\mu,\nu$ be finite nonatomic positive measures on $\mathbb R$, and set $M=\mu(\mathbb R)+\nu(\mathbb R)$. Let $A:L^2(\nu)\to L^2(\mu)$ have any one of the kernels $$\operatorname{sign}(s-t),\qquad
 \mathbf1_{s<t},\qquad \mathbf1_{s>t},$$ where $t$ is the output variable and $s$ the input variable. For each integer $N\ge1$, there is an operator $A_N$ of rank at most $N$ such that $$\|A-A_N\|\le \frac{M}{2N}.$$ Also $\|A\|\le\sqrt{\mu(\mathbb R)\nu(\mathbb R)}\le M/2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pf-order-kernel"><summary>Proof of stmt-pf-order-kernel</summary>

*Proof.* If $M=0$, the assertion is immediate. Otherwise partition $\mathbb R$ into $N$ successive intervals $I_1,\ldots,I_N$, each having $(\mu+\nu)$-measure $M/N$. Such a partition exists because the measure is nonatomic. Endpoint conventions do not affect the operators. Let $A_N$ retain the kernel of $A$ when $t$ and $s$ lie in different intervals, and set the kernel to zero when they lie in the same interval. On $I_a\times I_b$ with $a\ne b$, the order of $t$ and $s$ is fixed. The range of $A_N$ is therefore contained in the span of the $N$ functions $\mathbf1_{I_a}$ in $L^2(\mu)$.

The remainder $A-A_N$ maps $L^2(\nu|_{I_a})$ to $L^2(\mu|_{I_a})$ for each $a$, without mixing different intervals. On that block its kernel is bounded by one, so Cauchy–Schwarz gives the operator norm bound $$\sqrt{\mu(I_a)\nu(I_a)}
 \le \frac{\mu(I_a)+\nu(I_a)}2=\frac{M}{2N}.$$ Both the source blocks and the target blocks are orthogonal. Hence the norm of their direct sum is the maximum of their norms, proving the approximation claim. Applying the same Cauchy–Schwarz estimate on the whole line gives the asserted bound on $\|A\|$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pf-terminal-singular"></a>
<div id="pf:terminal-singular" class="lemma">

<a id="pf:singular-decay"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Singular values at the last stage). *For the operator $T_{b_*}$, every finite-dimensional orthogonal compression has singular values satisfying $$s_\nu\le \frac C\nu,\qquad \nu\ge1.
 \label{pf:singular-decay}$$ The constant is independent of $n,R,g$, and the tuple, in either of the two cases of Theorem <a href="#pf:bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pf:bound">18</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pf-terminal-singular"><summary>Proof of stmt-pf-terminal-singular</summary>

*Proof.* We will approximate $T_{b_*}$, for every $x>0$, by an operator of rank at most $C/x$ and norm error at most $x$.

First suppose that $g$ has infinite order. The final label set is $G$ and the label kernel is equality. It follows that $\mathcal H_{b_*}$ splits orthogonally according to the left $g$-orbits in $G$. In one orbit, choose a representative $y_0$ and write a label as $g^a y_0$, $a\in\mathbb Z$. Associate to the pair $(u,g^a y_0)$ the real coordinate $u-a$. If the second label is $g^b y_0$, the equality kernel in <a href="#pf:kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:kernel">[pf:kernel]</a> selects precisely the shift $a-b$. The resulting kernel is $$\operatorname{sign}(v+a-b-u)
 =\operatorname{sign}\bigl((v-b)-(u-a)\bigr).$$ Thus this orbit gives a sign operator on a nonatomic measure on the line. Let $b$ be the mass of that measure. The orbit masses sum to at most one, because the final channel is a subprobability channel. Lemma <a href="#pf:order-kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pf:order-kernel">19</a>, with the same measure in source and target, gives norm at most $b$ and a rank-$N$ approximation with error at most $b/N$.

Discard each orbit block with $b\le x$. On every other block choose $N=\lceil b/x\rceil$. The error of the resulting direct-sum approximation is at most $x$. Moreover, $$\sum_{b>x}\lceil b/x\rceil
 \le \frac1x\sum_{b>x}b+\#\{b>x\}
 \le \frac2x.$$ This proves the desired rank bound in the infinite-order case.

<a id="pf:three-shifts"></a>
Now suppose that $g$ has finite order. Put $d\mu_y(u)=Y_{b_*}(u)_y\,du$ on $(0,1)$, extend this measure by zero to $\mathbb R$, and write $\mathcal H_y=L^2(\mu_y)$. Since the final lag kernel is $\operatorname{sign}(s)\mathbf1_{|s|<1}$, the only possible shifts for $0<u,v<1$ are $0,1,-1$. The operator is the sum of the three kernels $$\begin{split}
  &\operatorname{sign}(v-u)\mathbf1_{y=z},\\
  &\mathbf1_{v<u}\mathbf1_{y=gz},\\
  &-\mathbf1_{v>u}\mathbf1_{y=g^{-1}z}.
 \end{split}
 \label{pf:three-shifts}$$ The shifted contributions are direct sums of maps $\mathcal H_z\to\mathcal H_{gz}$ and $\mathcal H_z\to\mathcal H_{g^{-1}z}$, respectively. After reindexing the target summands, each is an orthogonal direct sum: its source blocks are mutually orthogonal and its target blocks are mutually orthogonal. The identity contribution has the same property. This remains true even though the measures in a paired block need not coincide; the shifted summands need not be invariant subspaces.

For a paired block, let $b=\mu_y(\mathbb R)+\mu_z(\mathbb R)$. By Lemma <a href="#pf:order-kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pf:order-kernel">19</a>, its norm is at most $b$, and it has a rank-$N$ approximation with error at most $b/N$. The sums of these $b$’s over a single contribution are at most two: every label mass appears once as a source mass and once as a target mass. Discarding blocks of mass at most $\varepsilon$ and taking $N=\lceil b/\varepsilon\rceil$ on the others therefore gives error at most $\varepsilon$ and total rank at most $4/\varepsilon$. Use $\varepsilon=x/3$ for each contribution in <a href="#pf:three-shifts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:three-shifts">[pf:three-shifts]</a> and add the three approximations. Their total error is at most $x$ and their total rank is at most $C/x$. If $g$ has order two, the two shift permutations coincide; the separate estimates still apply and are added in exactly the same way.

Finally, orthogonal compression cannot increase either the rank or the error of an approximation. Singular values beyond the dimension of the compressed space are zero, so consider an index $\nu$ no larger than that dimension. If the compressed matrix has an approximation of rank less than $\nu$ with error $x$, its $\nu$th singular value is at most $x$. Indeed, the span of its first $\nu$ right singular vectors contains a unit vector annihilated by the approximating matrix, and the original matrix has norm at least $s_\nu$ on that vector. If the rank bound above is $C_0/x$, take $x=2C_0/\nu$. The approximating rank is then at most $\nu/2<\nu$, which proves <a href="#pf:singular-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:singular-decay">[pf:singular-decay]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pf:terminal"></a>
For an even principal set $I$ of size $k>0$, insert <a href="#pf:singular-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:singular-decay">[pf:singular-decay]</a> in <a href="#pf:compression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:compression">[pf:compression]</a>. Multiplication of the two-form by $W$ multiplies its size-$k$ Pfaffian by $W^{k/2}$. We obtain $$|\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}_I(WdB_{b_*})|
 \le W^{k/2}\left(\frac{a_{b_*}}{\sqrt{\delta_{b_*}}}\right)^k
       \frac{C^{k/2}}{\sqrt{k!}}\prod_{i\in I}l_i.$$ For $k\ge1$, integration of $\log x$ gives $\log(k!)\ge\int_1^k\log x\,dx\ge k\log k-k$, and hence $k!\ge(k/e)^k$. We conclude that $$|\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}_I(WdB_{b_*})|
 \le\bigl(Ca_{b_*}\sqrt W\,\beta_{b_*}^{-1/2}\bigr)^k
          (n/k)^{k/2}\prod_{i\in I}l_i.
 \label{pf:terminal}$$ The estimates <a href="#pf:intermediate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:intermediate">[pf:intermediate]</a> and <a href="#pf:terminal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:terminal">[pf:terminal]</a> control every smooth stage at every point of the simplex. It remains to control the raw stage in the average required for the full Pfaffian.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### The raw stage and nearby coordinates

Recall $J_i=\delta_{h_{i-1}}-\delta_{h_i}$. The coefficient of $dt_i$ in $B_0$ is $$-\frac12\int_{\mathbb R}U_0(v-t_i)K_0(J_i,Y_0(v))\,dv.$$ For distinct $i,i'$ in the interior of the simplex, differentiating at the boundary $t_{i'}$ and antisymmetrizing gives $$(dB_0)_{ii'}
 =\sum_{a\in\mathbb Z}U_0(t_{i'}+a-t_i)K_0(J_i,g^aJ_{i'}).$$ There is at most one nonzero summand. Since $K_0(h,h')=\chi_R(d(h,h'))$ and $\chi_R$ is $1/R$-Lipschitz, $$|K_0(J_i,g^aJ_{i'})|
 \le\frac{2d(h_{i-1},h_i)}R
 \le\frac{2l_i l_{i'}}R.$$ The support of $U_0$ also shows that this matrix entry is zero if the circular distance between $t_i$ and $t_{i'}$ exceeds $2R/n$.

<a id="pf:raw"></a>
For $I\subset\{1,\ldots,n\}$ and $i\in I$, let $N_i(I)$ be the number of indices in $I$, including $i$ itself, whose times have circular distance at most $2R/n$ from $t_i$. If $k=|I|$ is even, divide row and column $i$ of the principal curvature matrix by $l_i$. Each resulting row has Euclidean norm at most $(C/R)\sqrt{N_i(I)}$. Hadamard’s inequality, followed by the Pfaffian–determinant identity, yields $$|\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}_I(dB_0)|
 \le (C/R)^{k/2}\prod_{i\in I}l_i
                    \prod_{i\in I}N_i(I)^{1/4}.
 \label{pf:raw}$$

We next average the last product. The needed assertion is an average over subsets of a fixed size; it is not a bound asserted separately for each fixed subset of the ordered coordinates.

<a id="stmt-pf-occupancy"></a>
<div id="pf:occupancy" class="lemma">

<a id="pf:count-average"></a>
**Lemma 21** (Average of the neighbor counts). *Let $R\ge1$, $n\ge8R$, and $0\le k\le n$. Choose $(t_1,\ldots,t_n)$ with uniform probability on $\Delta_n$, and independently choose $I$ uniformly among the $k$-element subsets of $\{1,\ldots,n\}$. Then $$\mathbb E_{\Delta_n,I}\prod_{i\in I}N_i(I)^{1/4}
 \le C^k R^{k/4},
 \label{pf:count-average}$$ with the empty product interpreted as one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pf-occupancy"><summary>Proof of stmt-pf-occupancy</summary>

*Proof.* The statement is immediate for $k=0$. Uniform simplex coordinates are the order statistics of $n$ independent uniform points in $(0,1)$. Selecting a uniform set of $k$ ranks is equivalent, as an unordered set of points, to selecting a uniform set of $k$ of those independent points. The selected unordered set therefore has the law of $k$ independent uniform points on the circle. Notice that the fixed endpoint $t_0=0$ is not one of the selectable coordinates.

Partition the circle into $m'=\lfloor n/(2R)\rfloor$ equal cells. Their length is at least $2R/n$, and $m'\ge n/(4R)$. Let $p_a$ be the number of selected points in cell $a$, with cell indices read cyclically. A point in cell $a$ can have counted neighbors only in that cell or its two adjacent cells. Consequently $$\prod_{i\in I}N_i(I)^{1/4}
 \le\prod_{a:p_a>0}(p_a+p_{a-1}+p_{a+1})^{p_a/4}.$$ The comparison with the product of the occupancies themselves follows from $$\begin{split}
 \sum_{a:p_a>0}p_a
  \log\frac{p_a+p_{a-1}+p_{a+1}}{p_a}
 &\le\sum_{a:p_a>0}(p_{a-1}+p_{a+1})\\
 &\le2k.
 \end{split}$$ Thus the preceding product is at most $e^{k/2}\prod_a p_a^{p_a/4}$, with $0^0=1$.

The occupancy vector has multinomial probabilities $k!(m')^{-k}\prod_a(p_a!)^{-1}$. The factorial lower bound implies $p^{p/4}\le e^{p/4}(p!)^{1/4}$, so $$\mathbb E\prod_a p_a^{p_a/4}
 \le e^{k/4}\frac{k!}{(m')^k}
    \sum_{\sum p_a=k}\left(\prod_a\frac1{p_a!}\right)^{3/4}.$$ There are $\binom{k+m'-1}{k}$ nonnegative occupancy vectors. Applying Hölder’s inequality to the last sum, and then the multinomial theorem, gives $$\sum_{\sum p_a=k}\left(\prod_a\frac1{p_a!}\right)^{3/4}
 \le
 \left(\frac{(m')^k}{k!}\right)^{3/4}
 \binom{k+m'-1}{k}^{1/4}.$$ After substitution, the remaining factor apart from $C^k$ is $$\left[\frac{k!}{(m')^k}\binom{k+m'-1}{k}\right]^{1/4}
 =\left[\prod_{q=0}^{k-1}(1+q/m')\right]^{1/4}
 \le(1+k/m')^{k/4}.$$ Finally, $k\le n$ and $m'\ge n/(4R)$ give $k/m'\le4R$. Since $R\ge1$, all factors are bounded as in <a href="#pf:count-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:count-average">[pf:count-average]</a>. ◻

</details>

Combining <a href="#pf:raw" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:raw">[pf:raw]</a> with Lemma <a href="#pf:occupancy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pf:occupancy">21</a> gives a factor of size $(CR^{-1/4})^k$, once the length factors have been extracted and the coordinate subset has been averaged. The remaining task is to combine this subset average with the pointwise estimates for the smooth stages. We first make their scale dependence uniform.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Expansion of the normalized curvature

<a id="pf:stage-count"></a>
<a id="pf:small-base"></a>
First record the dependence of the preceding estimates on $R$. Since $r$ is a fixed multiple of $R$, the stopping condition compares $$\log\beta_j=(3/2)^{j-1}\log R,
 \qquad
