 \log(W^2R^{100})=O(R+\log R).$$ Taking $j$ of order $1+\log R$ makes the first expression at least the second. As in <a href="#co:stage-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:stage-count">[co:stage-count]</a>, minimality therefore gives $$b_*=O(1+\log R).
 \label{pf:stage-count}$$ For $j<J_*$, the constants $a_j$ are bounded independently of $R$, because this is a fixed finite set of stages. Their bases in <a href="#pf:intermediate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:intermediate">[pf:intermediate]</a> are at most $CR^{-1/8}$. At the later stages, $$a_j\le CR^{10}(1+b_*)^{20},
 \qquad \beta_j\ge R^{200},$$ so the intermediate bases are at most $CR^{-15}(1+\log R)^{20}$. At the last stage, the stopping condition $\beta_{b_*}\ge W^2R^{100}$ gives $$Ca_{b_*}\sqrt W\,\beta_{b_*}^{-1/2}
 \le CR^{-40}(1+\log R)^{20}W^{-1/2}.$$ Together with the raw factor just obtained, all these bases are at most $$\varepsilon_R=C_1R^{-1/10}\le1
 \label{pf:small-base}$$ for sufficiently large $R$, after choosing a fixed $C_1$ large enough.

<a id="pf:normalized-curvature"></a>
<a id="pf:rank-two"></a>
The normalization of the connection gives $$d\alpha=C_{\rm nor}^{-1}\sum_{j<b_*}dB_j
       +C_{\rm nor}^{-1}W\,dB_{b_*}+Q,
 \qquad
 Q=-C_{\rm nor}^{-2}dC_{\rm nor}\wedge B.
 \label{pf:normalized-curvature}$$ The lower bound $C_{\rm nor}\ge c_0>0$ from Proposition <a href="#co:connection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:connection">17</a> changes the bases in <a href="#pf:small-base" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:small-base">[pf:small-base]</a> only by a fixed factor; enlarge $C_1$ accordingly. The last term in <a href="#pf:normalized-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:normalized-curvature">[pf:normalized-curvature]</a> is decomposable, so $Q\wedge Q=0$. Moreover, <a href="#co:coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:coefficients">[co:coefficients]</a> implies $$|Q_{ii'}|\le C_Rl_i l_{i'}.
 \label{pf:rank-two}$$ In particular, this stage can contribute to a Pfaffian partition only on zero or two indices. The constant $C_R$ in <a href="#pf:rank-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:rank-two">[pf:rank-two]</a> is independent of $n$.

<a id="pf:partition-expansion"></a>
There are $S=b_*+2$ stages in <a href="#pf:normalized-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:normalized-curvature">[pf:normalized-curvature]</a>: the raw stage, the $b_*$ smooth stages, and $Q$. Denote their matrices by $M_0,\ldots,M_{S-1}$, with $M_{S-1}=Q$. The Pfaffian expansion is $$\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{S-1}M_j\right)
 =\sum_{I_0\sqcup\cdots\sqcup I_{S-1}=\{1,\ldots,n\}}
       \sigma(I_0,\ldots,I_{S-1})
       \prod_{j=0}^{S-1}\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}_{I_j}(M_j),
 \label{pf:partition-expansion}$$ where all $|I_j|$ are even and each sign $\sigma$ is $1$ or $-1$. There is no multiplicity factor for a fixed ordered partition. To check this normalization, expand the divided exterior power $(\sum_jM_j)^m/m!$ as $\sum_{\sum m_j=m}\prod_j M_j^{m_j}/m_j!$, and then extract the coordinate volume coefficient using <a href="#pf:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:normalization">[pf:normalization]</a> on each coordinate subset.

Take absolute values in <a href="#pf:partition-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:partition-expansion">[pf:partition-expansion]</a>. In each term the length factors multiply to $\prod_{i=1}^nl_i$, independently of the partition. Fix now the even sizes $k_j=|I_j|$. For any given raw subset $I_0$ of size $k_0$, the number of completions to a partition of these sizes is $$\frac{(n-k_0)!}{\prod_{j=1}^{S-1}k_j!},$$ which is independent of $I_0$. Every factor other than the raw neighbor counts has already been bounded independently of the simplex point and of the actual subset. Thus, upon summing over these partitions and integrating over the simplex, the count product in <a href="#pf:raw" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:raw">[pf:raw]</a> is averaged uniformly over all raw subsets of size $k_0$. Lemma <a href="#pf:occupancy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pf:occupancy">21</a> applies without any assertion about an individual fixed subset.

<a id="pf:entropy"></a>
The smooth estimates also contain factors $(n/k_j)^{k_j/4}$ or $(n/k_j)^{k_j/2}$. Their product is at most $$\prod_{j:k_j>0}(n/k_j)^{k_j/2}\le S^{n/2}.
 \label{pf:entropy}$$ For completeness, put $p_j=k_j/n$ on the positive sizes. Concavity of the logarithm gives $$\sum_jp_j\log(1/p_j)
 \le\log\left(\sum_jp_j/p_j\right)\le\log S;$$ exponentiating $n/2$ times this inequality proves <a href="#pf:entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:entropy">[pf:entropy]</a>. Factors for the raw and decomposable stages may be inserted here because they are at least one.

Let $q=k_{S-1}$. A nonzero term has $q=0$ or $q=2$. The small bases in the other stages give $\varepsilon_R^{n-q}$, while the decomposable stage costs at most $C_R$. Since $0<\varepsilon_R\le1$, both possibilities are bounded by $C_R\varepsilon_R^n$ after absorbing the single factor $\varepsilon_R^{-2}$ into $C_R$. This absorption introduces no dependence on $n$.

For fixed sizes, the number of ordered partitions is $n!/\prod_j k_j!$. Summed over all admissible sizes this is at most $S^n$, the number of all assignments of the $n$ indices to $S$ stages. Combining this count, <a href="#pf:entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:entropy">[pf:entropy]</a>, and the preceding uniform subset average proves $$\mathbb E_{\Delta_n}|\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}(d\alpha)|
 \le C_R\varepsilon_R^n S^{3n/2}\prod_{i=1}^n l_i.$$ Substitution of <a href="#pf:small-base" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:small-base">[pf:small-base]</a> proves <a href="#pf:main-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:main-estimate">[pf:main-estimate]</a>, and <a href="#pf:stage-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pf:stage-count">[pf:stage-count]</a> supplies the assertion about $S$. This completes the proof of Theorem <a href="#pf:bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pf:bound">18</a>.

<a id="sec:conclusion"></a>
### Proof of the trace-support theorem

We now sum the curvature estimate against the cyclic tuple weights. The order of the two limiting choices is essential: the spatial radius is fixed first, uniformly in the conjugacy class, and only then does the simplex dimension tend to infinity.

<details id="proof-stmt-pf-occupancy-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-pf-occupancy</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* By Proposition <a href="#red:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:reduction">3</a>, it suffices to treat a finitely generated group with a fixed word metric and an idempotent $p$ having an exponential moment. The zero idempotent is immediate, so assume $M_p>0$, with $M_p$ as in Lemma <a href="#cy:weights-lemma" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:weights-lemma">4</a>.

For the construction of Sections <a href="#sec:connection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:connection">5</a> and <a href="#sec:curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:curvature">6</a>, write $$Q_R=C S^{3/2}R^{-1/10},\qquad S=b_*+2.$$ The fixed constants here may depend on the word metric, but not on $g$, the tuple, or $n$. Since $S=O(1+\log R)$, we have $Q_R\to0$. Choose $R$ once and for all so large that the construction applies and <a id="final:radius-choice"></a>$$\label{final:radius-choice}
 Q_RM_p<1.$$ This fixes the final spatial radius $r=6^{J_*-1}R$, the scale ladder, and all constants denoted $C_R$.

<a id="final:decay"></a>
Let $g$ have infinite order, or have finite order with $\min_h d(h,gh)>4r$. Proposition <a href="#co:connection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:connection">17</a> provides a compatible connection family through every sufficiently large even dimension $n$. Combining Proposition <a href="#cy:trace-identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cy:trace-identity">6</a>, Theorem <a href="#pf:bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pf:bound">18</a>, and <a href="#cy:moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cy:moment">[cy:moment]</a>, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 |\tau_{[g]}(p)|
 &\le \sum_{h\in\mathcal T_n(g)}|v_{n+1}(h)|
               \mathbb E_{\Delta_n}|\mathop{\mathrm{Pf}}(d\alpha_h)|\notag\\
 &\le C_RQ_R^n\sum_{h\in\mathcal T_n(g)}|v_{n+1}(h)|
                                    \prod_{i=1}^n l_i\notag\\
 &\le C_RM_p\,(Q_RM_p)^n.\label{final:decay}
\end{aligned}$$ In the last step we used $l_0\ge1$ to insert the missing closing-edge factor. The constant $C_R$ is independent of $n$ and $g$. Letting $n$ tend to infinity through even integers in <a href="#final:decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="final:decay">[final:decay]</a> gives $\tau_{[g]}(p)=0$.

It remains to identify the possible surviving classes. Every infinite-order class has just been eliminated. If a finite-order class does not satisfy the displacement condition, some conjugate of its representative has word length at most $4r$, because $$d(h,gh)=|h^{-1}gh|.$$ Thus each surviving class meets the finite ball $B(1,4r)$. There are only finitely many such classes. This proves the result for $p$. Proposition <a href="#red:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:reduction">3</a> transfers it to the original $e$ and group. Finally, an element of $K_0(\ell^1(G))$ is a difference of idempotent classes; taking the union of their two finite supports proves the asserted inclusion for $\operatorname{HS}^1$. ◻

</details>
